Insurance English

An insurance English curriculum for underwriting, claims, policy language, coverage disputes, actuarial assumptions, broker communication, fraud concerns, compliance, and customer escalation.

For underwriters, claims adjusters, brokers, risk managers, actuarial analysts, policy-service teams, compliance staff, and insurance operations leaders.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Request material missing information while preserving the difference between review, quotation, and binding.
  • Explain the roles of policy documents without deciding coverage from an isolated heading.
  • Ask for precise missing claim information and distinguish an estimate from a settlement commitment.
  • Respond to pressure for certainty with an accurate status, specific outstanding issue, and useful follow-up.
  • Explain a renewal trade-off using a bounded deductible calculation and a complete comparison request.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

All insurers, policies, claims, people, figures, and local procedures are fictional. This is professional-English practice, not insurance, actuarial, legal, financial, or claims-handling advice. Actual wording, authority, notice requirements, privacy obligations, and complaint rights depend on the policy and applicable jurisdiction. No example binds coverage, determines a real claim, or prescribes an investigation.

01Underwriting and Risk SelectionAn incomplete submission cannot establish the risk+

Your goal

Request material missing information while preserving the difference between review, quotation, and binding.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

An incomplete submission cannot establish the risk

Broker Lena submits an application for Atlas Services, describing its activity only as maintenance. The application omits operating locations and the requested three-year loss history. Underwriter Theo does not know whether the work is performed at a workshop, at customer premises, or both. No quote, binder, or coverage confirmation has been issued. Lena will obtain the activity breakdown, location details, and loss records, and give Theo a status update Monday at 11:00. Atlas wants cover urgently, but neither the requested start date nor a complete submission automatically establishes acceptance or coverage.

Lena: Insurance broker / Theo: Underwriter

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which information is missing?

Answer and explanation

B. Operating locations and the requested three-year loss history

The application names Atlas and says maintenance, but omits the location details and requested loss history.

2. What does the blank loss-history field establish?

Answer and explanation

A. The information is missing.

A blank field provides no confirmed loss history and cannot be treated as a no-loss declaration.

3. What is committed for Monday at 11:00?

Answer and explanation

B. A submission-status update

Lena promises a status update, not underwriting acceptance or the start of coverage.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A complete submission is not the same as an acceptable risk, a quote, or bound insurance. Preserve the stage of the process. The urgency of a requested inception date does not itself create coverage.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
underwriting
Assessment and selection of risk under an insurer's applicable process.complete underwriting review
submission
Information supplied for an insurance assessment or quotation.prepare an underwriting submission
applicant
The person or organization requesting insurance.identify the applicant
business activity
The operations performed by the applicant.describe the business activity
risk appetite
The types and characteristics of risk an insurer is prepared to consider.assess fit with risk appetite
exposure
A condition or measure associated with the possibility of insured loss.identify the exposure
operating location
A place where the business carries out its activities.list operating locations
premises
Buildings or land occupied or used for an activity.describe the premises
off-site work
Work performed away from the business's own location.disclose off-site work
loss history
A record of prior losses or claims over a stated period.obtain the loss history
loss run
A report listing claims and related information for a defined account and period.request a loss run
valuation date
The date at which reported amounts or claim statuses are evaluated.state the valuation date
risk selection
Deciding which risks to accept under the relevant criteria.support risk selection
referral
Escalation to a person or level with the required authority.make an underwriting referral
underwriting authority
The defined power to make specified underwriting decisions.confirm underwriting authority
quote
Proposed insurance terms subject to their stated conditions and status.issue a quote
binder
A document or agreement providing temporary insurance as applicable to its terms.confirm the binder terms
inception date
The date on which insurance begins under the applicable agreement.verify the inception date
subjectivity
A stated requirement attached to a proposed underwriting arrangement.identify an underwriting subjectivity
material information
Information relevant under the applicable decision or disclosure framework.request material information
occupancy
The use of premises relevant to a property-risk assessment.describe the occupancy
risk engineering
Technical assessment of risk conditions and controls.request risk engineering input
declination
A decision not to offer the requested insurance.communicate a declination
submission completeness
Whether the requested submission information has been supplied.check submission completeness

Loss information needs a defined period and reporting basis. Missing records do not establish no losses. A loss run may show paid amounts, outstanding estimates, and status at a valuation date rather than a final outcome.

C Notice the language

Be specific without sounding obstructive

A broker under time pressure may hear more information required as a refusal. Identify the missing fields and why they matter to the review. Offer a concrete next contact while avoiding a promise that complete paperwork will produce a particular underwriting outcome.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Specify the missing information
We still need the operating locations and three-year loss history.
Clarify the activity
Does maintenance mean workshop work, customer-site work, or both?
Avoid a favorable assumption
A blank loss-history field does not mean no losses.
Name the information period
Please supply the requested three-year records.
Check the reporting basis
Include the loss run's valuation date.
Explain the purpose
These details are needed to assess the risk presented.
Separate the stages
Completing the submission does not guarantee acceptance.
Avoid a coverage promise
No quote, binder, or coverage confirmation has been issued.
Recognize urgency
I understand that the requested start date is important.
Keep the date accurate
A requested inception date is not a confirmed inception date.
Name the follow-up
Lena will obtain the activity and location breakdown.
Give a concrete contact
We will update the submission status Monday at 11:00.
Limit the commitment
That is an update time, not a promise of bound cover.
Check authority
Any decision must follow the applicable underwriting authority.
Preserve uncertainty
We have not established where all the work takes place.
Close the handoff
Send the missing records with their period and source clearly identified.
6 language and precision notes
Still need
Identifies an outstanding requirement without implying a refusal.
Does this mean
Requests clarification instead of selecting an unsupported interpretation.
No losses versus no records
Different factual statements that must not be substituted.
Requested versus confirmed
Distinguishes a desired date from an established agreement.
Complete versus accepted
Distinguishes information status from the underwriting decision.
Issued
Requires an actual document or decision, not an intention.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which request is most useful?

Answer and explanation

D. Please provide the operating locations, activity breakdown, and three-year loss records.

The useful request addresses all stated gaps; the alternatives repeat known information or supply an incomplete history.

2. Which statement improperly promises coverage?

Answer and explanation

A. Cover begins Monday because that is when the update is due.

The Monday commitment concerns information and does not establish coverage or underwriting acceptance.

3. What does supplying all requested records accomplish by itself?

Answer and explanation

A. It addresses submission completeness, not automatic acceptance.

Information completeness is a review milestone and does not itself determine the underwriting outcome.

4. Why ask for a loss run's valuation date?

Answer and explanation

A. Reported claim amounts and statuses relate to a stated evaluation date.

The valuation date provides context for the reported claim information rather than a universal final outcome.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

An incomplete submission cannot establish the risk

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Lena: Insurance broker / Theo: Underwriter

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • binder
  • submission
  • Submission completeness
  • underwriting authority
  • loss run
  • referral
  • operating location
  • inception date
  • business activity
  • loss history
  1. Lena
    Atlas needs cover quickly. The application says maintenance, but I can see the operating-location fields and three-year loss-history section are incomplete.
  2. Theo
    We need a clearer 1 description first. Does the work happen in a workshop, at customer premises, or both? We cannot choose one simply because the label sounds familiar.
  3. Lena
    I will obtain a breakdown from the client. The blank history field should not be treated as a statement that no losses occurred.
  4. Theo
    Correct. Request the 2 for the specified three years. Missing information is not a favorable answer. We need the actual record and any relevant explanation of its coverage or limitations.
  5. Lena
    The client may have records from its previous insurer. I will ask what period they cover and whether the reported amounts are current.
  6. Theo
    If a 3 is available, include its valuation date. Claim amounts and statuses can change. We should know the basis of the record rather than treat every figure as a final settled amount.
  7. Lena
    Atlas also has a storage address. I do not yet know whether work is performed there or whether it is only used for supplies.
  8. Theo
    Clarify each 4 and its use. An address alone does not tell us the occupancy or whether there is off-site work. The activity and location details need to agree.
  9. Lena
    Would complete records let Atlas tell its customer that insurance is arranged? They want a definite answer today.
  10. Theo
    No. 5 is one stage, not an acceptance decision. The information allows the risk to be reviewed under the applicable criteria and authority. It does not predetermine the outcome.
  11. Lena
    I will explain that distinction. The client requested Monday as the start date, but I have no document confirming that insurance begins then.
  12. Theo
    Keep the 6 described as requested. No quote, binder, or coverage confirmation has been issued. An urgent preference does not become confirmed insurance because we repeat it confidently.
  13. Lena
    I will call the date requested. Does this call provide temporary cover while the paperwork catches up?
  14. Theo
    Do not present it as a 7. This exchange requests information and describes review status. It does not create an insurance agreement or authorize you to represent that one has been issued.
  15. Lena
    I will update you Monday at eleven, even if a previous insurer has not yet supplied every record. Is that useful?
  16. Theo
    Yes. Give the 8 status and identify exactly what remains missing. A useful update can explain an unresolved dependency without disguising it as a completed review or an expected acceptance.
  17. Lena
    If the clarified activities require a more senior decision, I would rather know the next review step than make a promise to the client.
  18. Theo
    We can explain the relevant 9 status when applicable. Keep the distinction between a review route and a decision. Neither an escalation nor a request for information is automatically a declination.
  19. Lena
    My client update will list the requested facts, Monday's status contact, and the absence of any coverage confirmation. It will avoid a guaranteed outcome.
  20. Theo
    Good. Any eventual decision must respect 10 and the applicable process. Clear information helps that decision, but it does not substitute for the required authorization or the actual terms offered.

Answers and explanations
  1. business activity Turn 2. Business activity describes the actual operations; the broad word maintenance does not establish where or how Atlas works.
  2. loss history Turn 4. Loss history records prior losses over a stated period; an empty application field supplies no such evidence.
  3. loss run Turn 6. A loss run supplies claim information for an account and period; its evaluation date and contents still need to be understood.
  4. operating location Turn 8. Operating location identifies where activities occur; a storage address does not by itself establish the work performed there.
  5. Submission completeness Turn 10. Submission completeness concerns whether information is supplied; it is distinct from risk acceptance, quotation, or binding.
  6. inception date Turn 12. The inception date is established by the applicable insurance agreement, not merely by the applicant's preferred start date.
  7. binder Turn 14. A binder provides temporary insurance according to its applicable terms; this information-gathering conversation does not issue one.
  8. submission Turn 16. The submission is the information package being assessed; its status can be updated even when some records remain outstanding.
  9. referral Turn 18. Referral sends the issue to the required decision level; it does not itself mean the risk is accepted or declined.
  10. underwriting authority Turn 20. Underwriting authority defines who can make specified decisions; gathering complete information does not transfer that authority to every participant.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Lena: Atlas needs cover quickly. The application says maintenance, but I can see the operating-location fields and three-year loss-history section are incomplete.
  2. Theo: We need a clearer business activity description first. Does the work happen in a workshop, at customer premises, or both? We cannot choose one simply because the label sounds familiar.
  3. Lena: I will obtain a breakdown from the client. The blank history field should not be treated as a statement that no losses occurred.
  4. Theo: Correct. Request the loss history for the specified three years. Missing information is not a favorable answer. We need the actual record and any relevant explanation of its coverage or limitations.
  5. Lena: The client may have records from its previous insurer. I will ask what period they cover and whether the reported amounts are current.
  6. Theo: If a loss run is available, include its valuation date. Claim amounts and statuses can change. We should know the basis of the record rather than treat every figure as a final settled amount.
  7. Lena: Atlas also has a storage address. I do not yet know whether work is performed there or whether it is only used for supplies.
  8. Theo: Clarify each operating location and its use. An address alone does not tell us the occupancy or whether there is off-site work. The activity and location details need to agree.
  9. Lena: Would complete records let Atlas tell its customer that insurance is arranged? They want a definite answer today.
  10. Theo: No. Submission completeness is one stage, not an acceptance decision. The information allows the risk to be reviewed under the applicable criteria and authority. It does not predetermine the outcome.
  11. Lena: I will explain that distinction. The client requested Monday as the start date, but I have no document confirming that insurance begins then.
  12. Theo: Keep the inception date described as requested. No quote, binder, or coverage confirmation has been issued. An urgent preference does not become confirmed insurance because we repeat it confidently.
  13. Lena: I will call the date requested. Does this call provide temporary cover while the paperwork catches up?
  14. Theo: Do not present it as a binder. This exchange requests information and describes review status. It does not create an insurance agreement or authorize you to represent that one has been issued.
  15. Lena: I will update you Monday at eleven, even if a previous insurer has not yet supplied every record. Is that useful?
  16. Theo: Yes. Give the submission status and identify exactly what remains missing. A useful update can explain an unresolved dependency without disguising it as a completed review or an expected acceptance.
  17. Lena: If the clarified activities require a more senior decision, I would rather know the next review step than make a promise to the client.
  18. Theo: We can explain the relevant referral status when applicable. Keep the distinction between a review route and a decision. Neither an escalation nor a request for information is automatically a declination.
  19. Lena: My client update will list the requested facts, Monday's status contact, and the absence of any coverage confirmation. It will avoid a guaranteed outcome.
  20. Theo: Good. Any eventual decision must respect underwriting authority and the applicable process. Clear information helps that decision, but it does not substitute for the required authorization or the actual terms offered.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Which operations and locations are included?

Fictional underwriting intake: a submission says business services but omits operating locations and loss history. No coverage or acceptance decision is made.

  1. Underwriting assistant: The submission says business services. Could you clarify the activities and operating locations included?
  2. Broker account manager: The description is broad, and I haven't obtained the location schedule or loss history yet.
  3. Underwriting assistant: Those gaps affect the exposure information available for review. Who can provide the verified details?
  4. Broker account manager: I'll ask the client's authorized contact rather than assume all work happens at the registered address.
  5. Underwriting assistant: Thank you. Please distinguish current operations from any planned activities when obtaining the description.
  6. Broker account manager: Does requesting those details mean the risk already fits your underwriting appetite?
  7. Underwriting assistant: No. An information request isn't acceptance, a quote, or a coverage decision.
  8. Broker account manager: I'll keep that clear with the client and ask what loss-history records are available.
  9. Underwriting assistant: If any period is missing, identify it explicitly instead of describing the history as claim-free.
  10. Broker account manager: I'll return the verified activity and location details with any remaining gaps clearly marked for review.
Storage or manufacturing?

Separate fictional submission: a facility is described as a warehouse, but an attachment mentions assembly work; the extent of that activity is unspecified.

  1. Commercial underwriter: The form says warehouse, while the attachment mentions assembly. What activity actually occurs at the facility?
  2. Broker placement specialist: I need to clarify that with the client. The attachment doesn't describe the assembly process.
  3. Commercial underwriter: Please ask whether it's a current operation and which location the description concerns.
  4. Broker placement specialist: Would calling it minor assembly be enough to keep the exposure summary brief?
  5. Commercial underwriter: Not unless the client supplies facts supporting that description. Minor is too vague by itself.
  6. Broker placement specialist: I'll request a factual activity description and avoid choosing a label based on expected risk appetite.
  7. Commercial underwriter: Good. We need accurate information before considering how the submission fits our review criteria.
  8. Broker placement specialist: Should I tell the client that clarifying the activity guarantees a more favorable underwriting outcome?
  9. Commercial underwriter: No outcome is guaranteed. The purpose is a complete, accurate submission for assessment.
  10. Broker placement specialist: I'll explain that distinction and return with confirmed operations rather than an unsupported risk characterization.
A blank loss field is not a clean history

Separate fictional renewal submission: the loss-history field is blank; the broker has not established whether records are unavailable or no losses were reported.

  1. Underwriting coordinator: The loss-history field is blank. Does that mean no reported losses, or information not yet supplied?
  2. Broker service lead: We haven't established which. The client returned the form without completing that section.
  3. Underwriting coordinator: Please clarify the status and the period covered before we use it in the underwriting review.
  4. Broker service lead: Could I enter none temporarily so the application moves through the intake screen?
  5. Underwriting coordinator: No. That would turn missing information into a factual claim about the client's loss history.
  6. Broker service lead: I'll request the relevant records and identify any period the client cannot currently document.
  7. Underwriting coordinator: Thank you. Also avoid assuming a blank field establishes low exposure or automatic eligibility.
  8. Broker service lead: Understood. Is the immediate goal clarification, rather than asking the client to predict our decision?
  9. Underwriting coordinator: Exactly. We need evidence and an accurate status, not assurances about the eventual underwriting outcome.
  10. Broker service lead: I'll return a verified answer or explicitly state what remains missing for the review.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

An incomplete submission cannot establish the risk
  1. Read turns 1-10 and emphasize the activity, location, and loss-record requests.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the requested start date from bound cover.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Complete another submission handoff

The Nova application lacks two operating addresses and the requested two-year loss record. Priya will obtain the information. A status update is due Thursday at 10:00; no binder has been issued.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Broker: "The number of missing operating addresses is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. two

The supplied application lacks two addresses rather than a complete list of unknown size.

2. Underwriter: "The requested two-year document is the ___."

Answer and explanation

A. loss record

The brief identifies a two-year loss record as the other missing submission item.

3. Broker: "The person obtaining the information is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. Priya

Priya is explicitly responsible for obtaining the missing submission information.

4. Underwriter: "The status update is due ___."

Answer and explanation

B. Thursday at 10:00

Thursday at ten is an information-update commitment, not a confirmed coverage inception.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Insurance English
Professional audience: underwriters, claims adjusters, brokers, risk managers, actuarial analysts, policy-service teams, compliance staff, and insurance operations leaders
Scope: All insurers, policies, claims, people, figures, and local procedures are fictional. This is professional-English practice, not insurance, actuarial, legal, financial, or claims-handling advice. Actual wording, authority, notice requirements, privacy obligations, and complaint rights depend on the policy and applicable jurisdiction. No example binds coverage, determines a real claim, or prescribes an investigation.
Lesson 01: Underwriting and Risk Selection
Original fictional case: Broker Lena submits an application for Atlas Services, describing its activity only as maintenance. The application omits operating locations and the requested three-year loss history. Underwriter Theo does not know whether the work is performed at a workshop, at customer premises, or both. No quote, binder, or coverage confirmation has been issued. Lena will obtain the activity breakdown, location details, and loss records, and give Theo a status update Monday at 11:00. Atlas wants cover urgently, but neither the requested start date nor a complete submission automatically establishes acceptance or coverage.
Language workshop: An incomplete submission cannot establish the risk
Communication goal: Request material missing information while preserving the difference between review, quotation, and binding.
Language guidance: A complete submission is not the same as an acceptable risk, a quote, or bound insurance. Preserve the stage of the process. The urgency of a requested inception date does not itself create coverage.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We still need the operating locations and three-year loss history. / Does maintenance mean workshop work, customer-site work, or both? / A blank loss-history field does not mean no losses. / Please supply the requested three-year records. / Include the loss run's valuation date. / These details are needed to assess the risk presented. / Completing the submission does not guarantee acceptance. / No quote, binder, or coverage confirmation has been issued. / I understand that the requested start date is important. / A requested inception date is not a confirmed inception date. / Lena will obtain the activity and location breakdown. / We will update the submission status Monday at 11:00. / That is an update time, not a promise of bound cover. / Any decision must follow the applicable underwriting authority. / We have not established where all the work takes place. / Send the missing records with their period and source clearly identified.
Vocabulary: underwriting: Assessment and selection of risk under an insurer's applicable process. / submission: Information supplied for an insurance assessment or quotation. / applicant: The person or organization requesting insurance. / business activity: The operations performed by the applicant. / risk appetite: The types and characteristics of risk an insurer is prepared to consider. / exposure: A condition or measure associated with the possibility of insured loss. / operating location: A place where the business carries out its activities. / premises: Buildings or land occupied or used for an activity. / off-site work: Work performed away from the business's own location. / loss history: A record of prior losses or claims over a stated period. / loss run: A report listing claims and related information for a defined account and period. / valuation date: The date at which reported amounts or claim statuses are evaluated. / risk selection: Deciding which risks to accept under the relevant criteria. / referral: Escalation to a person or level with the required authority. / underwriting authority: The defined power to make specified underwriting decisions. / quote: Proposed insurance terms subject to their stated conditions and status. / binder: A document or agreement providing temporary insurance as applicable to its terms. / inception date: The date on which insurance begins under the applicable agreement. / subjectivity: A stated requirement attached to a proposed underwriting arrangement. / material information: Information relevant under the applicable decision or disclosure framework. / occupancy: The use of premises relevant to a property-risk assessment. / risk engineering: Technical assessment of risk conditions and controls. / declination: A decision not to offer the requested insurance. / submission completeness: Whether the requested submission information has been supplied.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 and emphasize the activity, location, and loss-record requests. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the requested start date from bound cover. Complete the Nova transfer and check the missing items, owner, and update time.
Partner: Lena: Insurance broker / Theo: Underwriter
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: The Nova application lacks two operating addresses and the requested two-year loss record. Priya will obtain the information. A status update is due Thursday at 10:00; no binder has been issued.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Lena: If the clarified activities require a more senior decision, I would rather know the next review step than make a promise to the client.
Theo: We can explain the relevant referral status when applicable. Keep the distinction between a review route and a decision. Neither an escalation nor a request for information is automatically a declination.
Lena: My client update will list the requested facts, Monday's status contact, and the absence of any coverage confirmation. It will avoid a guaranteed outcome.
Theo: Good. Any eventual decision must respect underwriting authority and the applicable process. Clear information helps that decision, but it does not substitute for the required authorization or the actual terms offered.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Insurance English
Professional audience: underwriters, claims adjusters, brokers, risk managers, actuarial analysts, policy-service teams, compliance staff, and insurance operations leaders
Scope: All insurers, policies, claims, people, figures, and local procedures are fictional. This is professional-English practice, not insurance, actuarial, legal, financial, or claims-handling advice. Actual wording, authority, notice requirements, privacy obligations, and complaint rights depend on the policy and applicable jurisdiction. No example binds coverage, determines a real claim, or prescribes an investigation.
Lesson 01: Underwriting and Risk Selection
Original fictional case: Broker Lena submits an application for Atlas Services, describing its activity only as maintenance. The application omits operating locations and the requested three-year loss history. Underwriter Theo does not know whether the work is performed at a workshop, at customer premises, or both. No quote, binder, or coverage confirmation has been issued. Lena will obtain the activity breakdown, location details, and loss records, and give Theo a status update Monday at 11:00. Atlas wants cover urgently, but neither the requested start date nor a complete submission automatically establishes acceptance or coverage.
Language workshop: An incomplete submission cannot establish the risk
Communication goal: Request material missing information while preserving the difference between review, quotation, and binding.
Language guidance: A complete submission is not the same as an acceptable risk, a quote, or bound insurance. Preserve the stage of the process. The urgency of a requested inception date does not itself create coverage.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We still need the operating locations and three-year loss history. / Does maintenance mean workshop work, customer-site work, or both? / A blank loss-history field does not mean no losses. / Please supply the requested three-year records. / Include the loss run's valuation date. / These details are needed to assess the risk presented. / Completing the submission does not guarantee acceptance. / No quote, binder, or coverage confirmation has been issued. / I understand that the requested start date is important. / A requested inception date is not a confirmed inception date. / Lena will obtain the activity and location breakdown. / We will update the submission status Monday at 11:00. / That is an update time, not a promise of bound cover. / Any decision must follow the applicable underwriting authority. / We have not established where all the work takes place. / Send the missing records with their period and source clearly identified.
Vocabulary: underwriting: Assessment and selection of risk under an insurer's applicable process. / submission: Information supplied for an insurance assessment or quotation. / applicant: The person or organization requesting insurance. / business activity: The operations performed by the applicant. / risk appetite: The types and characteristics of risk an insurer is prepared to consider. / exposure: A condition or measure associated with the possibility of insured loss. / operating location: A place where the business carries out its activities. / premises: Buildings or land occupied or used for an activity. / off-site work: Work performed away from the business's own location. / loss history: A record of prior losses or claims over a stated period. / loss run: A report listing claims and related information for a defined account and period. / valuation date: The date at which reported amounts or claim statuses are evaluated. / risk selection: Deciding which risks to accept under the relevant criteria. / referral: Escalation to a person or level with the required authority. / underwriting authority: The defined power to make specified underwriting decisions. / quote: Proposed insurance terms subject to their stated conditions and status. / binder: A document or agreement providing temporary insurance as applicable to its terms. / inception date: The date on which insurance begins under the applicable agreement. / subjectivity: A stated requirement attached to a proposed underwriting arrangement. / material information: Information relevant under the applicable decision or disclosure framework. / occupancy: The use of premises relevant to a property-risk assessment. / risk engineering: Technical assessment of risk conditions and controls. / declination: A decision not to offer the requested insurance. / submission completeness: Whether the requested submission information has been supplied.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 and emphasize the activity, location, and loss-record requests. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the requested start date from bound cover. Complete the Nova transfer and check the missing items, owner, and update time.
Partner: Lena: Insurance broker / Theo: Underwriter
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: The Nova application lacks two operating addresses and the requested two-year loss record. Priya will obtain the information. A status update is due Thursday at 10:00; no binder has been issued.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Lena: If the clarified activities require a more senior decision, I would rather know the next review step than make a promise to the client.
Theo: We can explain the relevant referral status when applicable. Keep the distinction between a review route and a decision. Neither an escalation nor a request for information is automatically a declination.
Lena: My client update will list the requested facts, Monday's status contact, and the absence of any coverage confirmation. It will avoid a guaranteed outcome.
Theo: Good. Any eventual decision must respect underwriting authority and the applicable process. Clear information helps that decision, but it does not substitute for the required authorization or the actual terms offered.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

02Policy Language and Coverage InterpretationA summary does not contain the whole contract+

Your goal

Explain the roles of policy documents without deciding coverage from an isolated heading.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A summary does not contain the whole contract

Business owner Rosa has a one-page summary showing a property limit of $500,000 and a $2,000 deductible. She assumes that every type of property damage is covered up to that limit. Account specialist Evan has the declarations and base form, but the listed endorsement E17 has not yet been retrieved. The full policy also contains conditions and exclusions that require review. No particular loss is being decided in this exchange. Evan will obtain E17 and walk Rosa through the applicable documents; the limit and deductible alone do not establish whether any specific event is covered.

Rosa: Business owner / Evan: Account specialist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the one-page summary show?

Answer and explanation

C. A $500,000 property limit and a $2,000 deductible

The summary contains the stated limit and deductible, not the entire set of policy terms.

2. Which listed document is missing from the current review?

Answer and explanation

B. Endorsement E17

Evan has not yet retrieved the listed endorsement E17.

3. What is not established by the two amounts alone?

Answer and explanation

C. Coverage for a particular event

Limits and deductibles describe terms but do not by themselves establish coverage for a specific event.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An endorsement can add, restrict, or otherwise modify coverage; it is not automatically an extra benefit. Its form, effective date, and actual wording matter. E17 cannot be interpreted before its text is obtained.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
policy wording
The text establishing the insurance contract's applicable terms.review the policy wording
declarations
Policy information identifying the insured, period, limits, and other stated particulars.check the declarations
insuring agreement
The policy section describing the basic insurance promise subject to its terms.read the insuring agreement
definition
A specified meaning assigned to a term in the policy.apply the policy definition
condition
A requirement or provision affecting rights and obligations under the policy.review a policy condition
exclusion
A provision removing specified matters from coverage, subject to the applicable wording.examine an exclusion
endorsement
A document modifying the policy's terms.read the applicable endorsement
schedule
A list of items, locations, amounts, or forms attached to a policy.check the policy schedule
coverage limit
The maximum applicable amount subject to the policy's terms.identify the coverage limit
sublimit
A separate lower limit applying to a specified part of coverage.identify a sublimit
aggregate limit
A limit applying to a defined total of claims or losses over a stated basis.review the aggregate limit
per-occurrence limit
A limit applying to one occurrence as defined by the policy.check the per-occurrence limit
deductible
The specified amount or share borne by the insured under the applicable terms.explain the deductible
retention
A portion of risk or cost retained under a defined arrangement.distinguish a retention
named insured
A person or organization identified as an insured in the policy.verify the named insured
additional insured
A party given insured status for a defined scope under the applicable terms.confirm additional insured status
covered property
Property meeting the policy's applicable coverage description.identify covered property
covered cause of loss
A cause of loss within the applicable coverage wording.assess a covered cause of loss
policy period
The interval during which the policy applies according to its terms.confirm the policy period
effective date
The date on which a specified policy or change takes effect.verify the endorsement's effective date
form number
An identifier for a policy document or wording version.match the form number
coverage summary
A condensed description that may omit detailed policy provisions.qualify the coverage summary
interpretation
Explanation of wording in its full relevant context.support the interpretation
document set
The complete applicable collection of policy forms and amendments.assemble the document set

A limit is not a guarantee that every loss is covered or paid in full. The deductible also does not answer coverage by itself. Definitions, insuring terms, exclusions, conditions, endorsements, and facts must be considered as applicable.

C Notice the language

Translate structure before offering interpretation

Clients may reasonably expect a short summary to be complete. Explain which document supplies which information and why the applicable forms must be read together. Avoid blaming the client for an inference encouraged by an oversimplified summary.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Recognize the inference
I can see why the short summary gave that impression.
Name the document's role
This is a summary, not the complete policy wording.
State the visible amounts
It shows a $500,000 limit and a $2,000 deductible.
Limit the conclusion
Those amounts do not establish coverage for every event.
Identify the missing form
Endorsement E17 is listed but has not been retrieved.
Avoid guessing the change
We cannot infer E17's effect from its number.
Read the set together
We need the applicable forms and endorsements.
Explain an endorsement
It can modify coverage, including restricting it.
Keep definitions visible
The policy may assign a specific meaning to a familiar word.
Check the applicable period
Confirm the form and effective date.
Avoid a blanket promise
I cannot describe every type of damage as covered.
Distinguish limit and scope
The amount of a limit and the scope of coverage are different questions.
Offer a concrete next step
I will obtain E17 and review the document set with you.
Preserve the actual task
We are clarifying documents, not deciding a particular loss today.
Ask a precise question
Which wording supports the coverage statement in the summary?
Close accurately
We will explain the applicable provisions together rather than rely on one heading.
6 language and precision notes
Subject to
Connects a statement to qualifications that may materially affect it.
Listed versus retrieved
A document reference is not the document's actual wording.
Can modify
Includes additions and restrictions, not only extra protection.
Every type
An absolute claim unsupported by a summary heading.
Applicable
Restricts the review to the correct forms and period.
Limit versus coverage
The maximum amount and the existence of protection are separate questions.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. The summary shows amounts but does not contain every applicable term.

The summary is condensed, while the missing endorsement and other policy provisions still require review.

2. What should Evan do about E17?

Answer and explanation

C. Obtain the applicable text and check its form and effective date.

The actual applicable endorsement must be reviewed rather than guessed from its identifier or another policy.

3. Which statement confuses amount and scope?

Answer and explanation

B. A $500,000 limit proves that any property loss is insured.

The maximum stated amount does not establish that every cause or type of loss falls within coverage.

4. Why review policy definitions?

Answer and explanation

D. Familiar words can have specified meanings in the contract.

Contract definitions can narrow or specify ordinary words and must be read with the applicable provisions.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

A summary does not contain the whole contract

Answers and explanations
  1. coverage summary Turn 2. A coverage summary is condensed and may omit provisions needed to understand the actual scope of protection.
  2. document set Turn 4. The document set includes the relevant policy forms and amendments, not merely the convenient summary and declarations.
  3. endorsement Turn 6. An endorsement modifies policy terms and can add, restrict, or otherwise change coverage depending on its wording.
  4. insuring agreement Turn 8. The insuring agreement describes the basic promise, but it operates with the policy's other applicable provisions.
  5. definition Turn 10. A policy definition assigns a specified meaning that may differ from everyday usage and affects interpretation in context.
  6. deductible Turn 12. A deductible specifies an insured share under the terms; it does not create coverage for an otherwise uncovered event.
  7. coverage limit Turn 14. A coverage limit is a maximum subject to terms, not a guaranteed payment or proof that every loss is covered.
  8. exclusion Turn 16. An exclusion removes specified matters from coverage according to its wording; it must be read in the complete applicable context.
  9. form number Turn 18. The form number identifies a document or version, helping confirm that the reviewed wording belongs to the applicable policy set.
  10. policy wording Turn 20. Policy wording establishes the relevant terms; reviewing it is different from making a coverage decision about an unspecified loss.
Read the complete conversation

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A summary does not contain every policy term

Fictional policy-document discussion: a summary is available alongside a full policy with endorsements and exclusions; no specific loss is interpreted.

  1. Broker account executive: The client thinks this one-page summary contains every term governing their coverage.
  2. Policy service specialist: We should explain that it summarizes information, while the full policy includes additional wording and documents.
  3. Broker account executive: How would you explain an endorsement without giving a legal interpretation of this client's policy?
  4. Policy service specialist: It's a document that changes policy terms; the actual wording must be reviewed with the policy.
  5. Broker account executive: Then the absence of an exclusion from the summary doesn't prove that exclusion is absent entirely.
  6. Policy service specialist: Correct. We need the complete applicable documents before a qualified reviewer addresses a specific coverage question.
  7. Broker account executive: Can I reassure the client that the summary overrides any conflicting detail in the full documents?
  8. Policy service specialist: No. We shouldn't invent a rule of precedence or determine their legal position here.
  9. Broker account executive: I'll explain the summary's limits and help identify the full document set for review.
  10. Policy service specialist: Good. That clarifies the document distinction without promising coverage or interpreting a particular disputed term.
A certificate is not the whole contract

Separate fictional document-coordination case: a client provides a certificate as proof of detailed coverage terms, but the applicable policy wording has not been reviewed.

  1. Client insurance coordinator: The certificate lists an insurance limit. Does that tell us every condition for a future claim?
  2. Broker documentation specialist: No. A certificate isn't the complete policy wording, and the stated limit alone doesn't settle coverage.
  3. Client insurance coordinator: Could you explain the difference in plain language for our contract-management meeting today?
  4. Broker documentation specialist: One document provides selected insurance information; the policy and relevant endorsements contain the terms needing review.
  5. Client insurance coordinator: So a number on the certificate shouldn't be described as a guaranteed payment amount.
  6. Broker documentation specialist: Correct. A particular claim depends on the applicable documents and facts assessed through the proper process.
  7. Client insurance coordinator: Should we ask for the full relevant wording instead of trying to infer exclusions from the certificate?
  8. Broker documentation specialist: Yes, using the authorized document-sharing channel and an appropriately qualified reviewer for interpretation.
  9. Client insurance coordinator: I'll describe the certificate as limited information and keep the contract's insurance questions open.
  10. Broker documentation specialist: That is clearer than treating one document as a substitute for the complete coverage review.
A requested endorsement has not been issued

Separate fictional policy-service case: a broker requested a location endorsement, but no issued endorsement or confirmed effective date is available.

  1. Broker service manager: We requested the location endorsement. Can I tell the client the policy has already changed?
  2. Policy administrator: Not from the request alone. We haven't received an issued endorsement or confirmed effective date.
  3. Broker service manager: I thought endorsement request and endorsement were interchangeable because they refer to the same change.
  4. Policy administrator: The request asks for a change; an issued document records a change subject to its actual terms.
  5. Broker service manager: Then our status should distinguish requested wording from confirmed policy documentation for the client.
  6. Policy administrator: Exactly. Don't infer coverage for that location while the relevant status remains unverified.
  7. Broker service manager: Could I use the date we submitted the request as the effective date in our summary?
  8. Policy administrator: No. Submission timing doesn't establish the effective date of an approved change.
  9. Broker service manager: I'll ask for the authorized status and tell the client the endorsement remains requested.
  10. Policy administrator: Good. Once the documents arrive, the relevant wording and timing can be reviewed appropriately.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A summary does not contain the whole contract
  1. Read turns 1-10 and distinguish the summary, declarations, base form, and endorsement.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; say what the deductible and limit do not establish.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Identify another incomplete document set

A summary shows a $200,000 limit and a $1,500 deductible. The declarations list endorsement R4, which has not been retrieved. No specific claim is under decision.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Client: "The displayed limit is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

C. 200,000

Two hundred thousand is the stated limit, not a guaranteed payment for every loss.

2. Specialist: "The displayed deductible is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. 1,500

Fifteen hundred is the deductible shown in the supplied summary.

3. Client: "The missing endorsement is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. R4

R4 is the listed endorsement whose actual text has not been obtained.

4. Specialist: "A specific claim is ___ under decision here."

Answer and explanation

A. not

The case explicitly concerns documents rather than a particular claim decision.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 02: Policy Language and Coverage Interpretation
Original fictional case: Business owner Rosa has a one-page summary showing a property limit of $500,000 and a $2,000 deductible. She assumes that every type of property damage is covered up to that limit. Account specialist Evan has the declarations and base form, but the listed endorsement E17 has not yet been retrieved. The full policy also contains conditions and exclusions that require review. No particular loss is being decided in this exchange. Evan will obtain E17 and walk Rosa through the applicable documents; the limit and deductible alone do not establish whether any specific event is covered.
Language workshop: A summary does not contain the whole contract
Communication goal: Explain the roles of policy documents without deciding coverage from an isolated heading.
Language guidance: An endorsement can add, restrict, or otherwise modify coverage; it is not automatically an extra benefit. Its form, effective date, and actual wording matter. E17 cannot be interpreted before its text is obtained.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I can see why the short summary gave that impression. / This is a summary, not the complete policy wording. / It shows a $500,000 limit and a $2,000 deductible. / Those amounts do not establish coverage for every event. / Endorsement E17 is listed but has not been retrieved. / We cannot infer E17's effect from its number. / We need the applicable forms and endorsements. / It can modify coverage, including restricting it. / The policy may assign a specific meaning to a familiar word. / Confirm the form and effective date. / I cannot describe every type of damage as covered. / The amount of a limit and the scope of coverage are different questions. / I will obtain E17 and review the document set with you. / We are clarifying documents, not deciding a particular loss today. / Which wording supports the coverage statement in the summary? / We will explain the applicable provisions together rather than rely on one heading.
Vocabulary: policy wording: The text establishing the insurance contract's applicable terms. / declarations: Policy information identifying the insured, period, limits, and other stated particulars. / insuring agreement: The policy section describing the basic insurance promise subject to its terms. / definition: A specified meaning assigned to a term in the policy. / condition: A requirement or provision affecting rights and obligations under the policy. / exclusion: A provision removing specified matters from coverage, subject to the applicable wording. / endorsement: A document modifying the policy's terms. / schedule: A list of items, locations, amounts, or forms attached to a policy. / coverage limit: The maximum applicable amount subject to the policy's terms. / sublimit: A separate lower limit applying to a specified part of coverage. / aggregate limit: A limit applying to a defined total of claims or losses over a stated basis. / per-occurrence limit: A limit applying to one occurrence as defined by the policy. / deductible: The specified amount or share borne by the insured under the applicable terms. / retention: A portion of risk or cost retained under a defined arrangement. / named insured: A person or organization identified as an insured in the policy. / additional insured: A party given insured status for a defined scope under the applicable terms. / covered property: Property meeting the policy's applicable coverage description. / covered cause of loss: A cause of loss within the applicable coverage wording. / policy period: The interval during which the policy applies according to its terms. / effective date: The date on which a specified policy or change takes effect. / form number: An identifier for a policy document or wording version. / coverage summary: A condensed description that may omit detailed policy provisions. / interpretation: Explanation of wording in its full relevant context. / document set: The complete applicable collection of policy forms and amendments.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 and distinguish the summary, declarations, base form, and endorsement. Swap roles for turns 11-20; say what the deductible and limit do not establish. Complete the R4 transfer and check the two amounts, missing form, and claim status.
Partner: Rosa: Business owner / Evan: Account specialist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A summary shows a $200,000 limit and a $1,500 deductible. The declarations list endorsement R4, which has not been retrieved. No specific claim is under decision.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: Is the effective date another detail we should check when the endorsement arrives? I do not want to use an unrelated version.
Evan: Yes. Match the form number and effective date to the applicable policy. A similar document from another period or account is not a reliable substitute for the actual issued wording.
Rosa: Please obtain E17 and walk me through the set. We are not asking you to decide a specific claim in this conversation.
Evan: Agreed. We will explain the policy wording together and correct the summary's overbroad impression. Any particular loss would require its facts and the applicable decision process, not a conclusion from the two amounts alone.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
