03Claims Intake and ReservingOpen the file without inventing the loss details+

Your goal

Ask for precise missing claim information and distinguish an estimate from a settlement commitment.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Open the file without inventing the loss details

Intake specialist Mina receives notice of alleged water damage under claim C204. The caller, Ben, has provided contact details and photographs, but the event date is unknown and the repair estimate has not been received. The date the damage was discovered is 6 June; that is not necessarily the date the event occurred. An adjuster has not completed the coverage or amount review. Mina will request the known timeline and supporting documents through the approved channel. An internal reserve, if established under the applicable process, is an estimate for claim accounting and management, not a promise of payment to Ben.

Ben: Policyholder / Mina: Claims intake specialist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What date is known?

Answer and explanation

A. Discovery on 6 June

Six June is the discovery date; the brief explicitly leaves the event date unknown.

2. What supporting document is missing?

Answer and explanation

D. The repair estimate

Photographs and contact details are supplied, but the repair estimate has not arrived.

3. What does an internal reserve not establish?

Answer and explanation

A. A payment promise to Ben

A reserve is an internal estimate and does not itself approve coverage or promise settlement payment.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The event date, discovery date, and notification date can differ. Record the known discovery date as such. Do not move it into the event-date field merely because the latter is empty.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
first notice of loss (FNOL)
The initial report of an event or loss to an insurer.record first notice of loss
claimant
A person or entity making a claim.identify the claimant
policyholder
The person or organization holding the policy.contact the policyholder
claim number
The identifier assigned to a claim file.quote the claim number
date of loss
The date associated with the event causing the claimed loss, as applicable.establish the date of loss
discovery date
The date the loss or damage was first noticed.record the discovery date
notification date
The date the insurer or relevant recipient was notified.confirm the notification date
claim intake
The initial collection and recording of claim information.complete claim intake
adjuster
A person responsible for investigating or evaluating claims within their role.assign an adjuster
supporting documentation
Records relevant to evaluating the claimed event or amount.request supporting documentation
repair estimate
A projected cost for specified repair work.obtain a repair estimate
proof of loss
A statement or document describing claimed loss as required by the applicable process.request the applicable proof of loss
case reserve
An estimate assigned to an individual claim under the reserving process.review the case reserve
unpaid claim estimate
An estimate of amounts remaining to be paid on relevant claims.assess an unpaid claim estimate
paid loss
Claim amounts already paid on the stated basis.report paid loss
incurred loss
A loss measure combining paid amounts and relevant estimates on a specified basis.define the incurred loss basis
incurred but not reported (IBNR)
An estimate for claims or development not yet reflected in reported information on the stated basis.explain the IBNR estimate
loss adjustment expense (LAE)
Costs associated with investigating and settling claims under the accounting definition used.distinguish loss adjustment expense
settlement
An agreement or resolution concerning the claim under applicable terms.document a settlement
payment authorization
Approval for a specified claim payment.verify payment authorization
claim chronology
A timeline of reported events and claim-handling steps.build a claim chronology
information request
A request specifying what additional material is needed.issue a clear information request
secure submission channel
An approved route for transmitting sensitive claim information.use the secure submission channel
acknowledgment
Confirmation that a report or communication was received.send a claim acknowledgment

Reserving estimates and settlement decisions serve different purposes. A reserve may change as information develops; it is not a contractual coverage determination or an amount the policyholder has been promised.

C Notice the language

Let the client say unknown

People sometimes guess to satisfy a form or to avoid sounding unhelpful. Ask for what is known, distinguish discovery from occurrence, and record uncertainty accurately. A complete-looking file is less useful than an honest file that identifies the missing evidence.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge receipt
We have opened claim C204 and received your photographs.
Separate the dates
You discovered the damage on 6 June.
Preserve the unknown
The event date has not yet been established.
Avoid a guessed entry
Please do not guess a date to fill the form.
Request a known timeline
Tell us when you first noticed the damage and what records you have.
Identify the missing document
We still need the repair estimate.
Explain the purpose
The adjuster needs these details to evaluate the reported loss.
Protect the channel
Please use the approved submission route for the documents.
Confirm what is already held
We have your contact details and photographs.
Avoid repeated requests
I will check the file before asking you to resend anything.
Keep the claim reference
Include C204 with the supporting material.
State the review stage
Coverage and amount reviews are not complete.
Explain an internal estimate
A reserve is not a promise of payment.
Separate authorization
No payment is authorized by this intake conversation.
Document uncertainty
I will record the date as unknown rather than inferred.
Close the request
We need the known timeline and repair estimate, not an invented event date.
6 language and precision notes
Discovered on
States when damage was noticed without dating the underlying event.
Not established
Preserves an unresolved fact rather than inserting a guess.
Received versus reviewed
Possession of a document does not establish a completed evaluation.
Estimate versus promise
A projected amount is not necessarily a payment commitment.
Still need
Should identify a specific missing item, not demand everything again.
As applicable
Recognizes that requirements depend on the actual policy and process.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which entry accurately records 6 June?

Answer and explanation

D. Discovery date

Six June is when Ben noticed the damage; the event's actual date remains unknown.

2. Which follow-up request is best?

Answer and explanation

D. Please provide the known timeline and repair estimate through the approved channel, quoting C204.

The complete request addresses the missing evidence while preserving the unknown event date and correct file reference.

3. Which statement improperly equates reserve and payment?

Answer and explanation

B. The internal reserve is the amount we promise to pay you.

A reserve is an internal estimate, not a settlement agreement or payment authorization.

4. What should Mina do before asking for photographs again?

Answer and explanation

C. Check the file, which already contains photographs.

The brief confirms photographs are received, so checking the file avoids an unnecessary duplicate request.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Open the file without inventing the loss details

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Policyholder / Mina: Claims intake specialist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • first notice of loss
  • case reserve
  • discovery date
  • repair estimate
  • acknowledgment
  • claim chronology
  • adjuster
  • secure submission channel
  • Payment authorization
  • information request
  1. Ben
    I sent photographs, but I only know the discovery date, not when the event occurred.
  2. Mina
    We have your 1 under C204. Please tell me what you know without guessing. The report can accurately identify an unresolved fact rather than present an unsupported date as certain.
  3. Ben
    I noticed the damage on the sixth of June. I cannot say whether the underlying event happened that morning or earlier.
  4. Mina
    I will record that as the 2. The event date remains unknown. Those fields answer different questions, so I will not copy the sixth into the event-date field merely to complete it.
  5. Ben
    Your reminder asks for documents, but I sent the photographs. What is still missing?
  6. Mina
    We still need the 3. Your contact details and photographs are already in the file. I will make the follow-up specific rather than ask you to resend everything without checking what we hold.
  7. Ben
    The repair company has not finished its estimate. I can send it when available, but I cannot provide a reliable amount today.
  8. Mina
    Note that status in the 4. We need the known timeline and any relevant records, with uncertainties identified. An unavailable estimate should not become a made-up amount simply because the file has a field for it.
  9. Ben
    Would sending the estimate mean the claim is accepted? I need to explain the next step.
  10. Mina
    The 5 still needs to review the claim. Providing a document is not the same as a coverage decision or agreement on the amount. Those reviews are not complete in this case.
  11. Ben
    Someone mentioned a reserve in an internal note. Is that the amount the insurer has promised to pay me for the damage?
  12. Mina
    A 6 is an internal estimate under the applicable process, not a settlement promise. It may change as information develops and does not by itself establish coverage or the final amount payable.
  13. Ben
    Understood. I should not tell the repairer that the reserve is approved payment. We have no settlement yet.
  14. Mina
    Correct. 7 is a separate matter. This intake conversation collects information and explains status. It does not authorize a payment or replace the review of the applicable policy and facts.
  15. Ben
    How should I send the repair estimate and timeline? They include some personal contact information and the property reference.
  16. Mina
    Use the approved 8 provided through our verified service process, and include claim C204. Do not send sensitive documents to an unverified address simply because a message appears to mention your claim.
  17. Ben
    Please make the request list clear. I will provide the known dates and the repair estimate when it is available, without guessing the event date.
  18. Mina
    I will send that 9 and identify what we already hold. This helps you respond to the actual gaps rather than repeat work or infer that every earlier submission has been lost.
  19. Ben
    That gives me a practical next step. I understand that receipt of the documents is not confirmation of coverage or the final settlement amount.
  20. Mina
    Exactly. Our 10 will confirm receipt, while the review status remains separate. Keep the claim reference on future correspondence so the new information can be connected to the correct file and evaluated in context.

Answers and explanations
  1. first notice of loss Turn 2. First notice of loss records the initial claim report; receiving it does not establish all event facts or coverage.
  2. discovery date Turn 4. The discovery date is when damage was noticed; it is not necessarily when the event causing it occurred.
  3. repair estimate Turn 6. The repair estimate is the specific missing cost document; photographs have already been received and should not be requested indiscriminately again.
  4. claim chronology Turn 8. The claim chronology records the actual sequence and outstanding information without inventing an amount or event date.
  5. adjuster Turn 10. The adjuster evaluates the claim within the assigned role; receiving an estimate does not itself complete the coverage or amount review.
  6. case reserve Turn 12. A case reserve is an individual-claim estimate under the internal process, not a payment promise or coverage determination.
  7. Payment authorization Turn 14. Payment authorization approves a specified payment; an internal estimate or intake conversation does not supply that approval.
  8. secure submission channel Turn 16. The secure submission channel is the authorized route for sensitive material; the example does not ask for disclosure through an unverified address.
  9. information request Turn 18. The information request should specify the missing timeline and estimate, preserving known facts and the unresolved event date.
  10. acknowledgment Turn 20. An acknowledgment confirms receipt, not acceptance of coverage, agreement on the loss amount, or authorization of payment.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: I sent photographs, but I only know the discovery date, not when the event occurred.
  2. Mina: We have your first notice of loss under C204. Please tell me what you know without guessing. The report can accurately identify an unresolved fact rather than present an unsupported date as certain.
  3. Ben: I noticed the damage on the sixth of June. I cannot say whether the underlying event happened that morning or earlier.
  4. Mina: I will record that as the discovery date. The event date remains unknown. Those fields answer different questions, so I will not copy the sixth into the event-date field merely to complete it.
  5. Ben: Your reminder asks for documents, but I sent the photographs. What is still missing?
  6. Mina: We still need the repair estimate. Your contact details and photographs are already in the file. I will make the follow-up specific rather than ask you to resend everything without checking what we hold.
  7. Ben: The repair company has not finished its estimate. I can send it when available, but I cannot provide a reliable amount today.
  8. Mina: Note that status in the claim chronology. We need the known timeline and any relevant records, with uncertainties identified. An unavailable estimate should not become a made-up amount simply because the file has a field for it.
  9. Ben: Would sending the estimate mean the claim is accepted? I need to explain the next step.
  10. Mina: The adjuster still needs to review the claim. Providing a document is not the same as a coverage decision or agreement on the amount. Those reviews are not complete in this case.
  11. Ben: Someone mentioned a reserve in an internal note. Is that the amount the insurer has promised to pay me for the damage?
  12. Mina: A case reserve is an internal estimate under the applicable process, not a settlement promise. It may change as information develops and does not by itself establish coverage or the final amount payable.
  13. Ben: Understood. I should not tell the repairer that the reserve is approved payment. We have no settlement yet.
  14. Mina: Correct. Payment authorization is a separate matter. This intake conversation collects information and explains status. It does not authorize a payment or replace the review of the applicable policy and facts.
  15. Ben: How should I send the repair estimate and timeline? They include some personal contact information and the property reference.
  16. Mina: Use the approved secure submission channel provided through our verified service process, and include claim C204. Do not send sensitive documents to an unverified address simply because a message appears to mention your claim.
  17. Ben: Please make the request list clear. I will provide the known dates and the repair estimate when it is available, without guessing the event date.
  18. Mina: I will send that information request and identify what we already hold. This helps you respond to the actual gaps rather than repeat work or infer that every earlier submission has been lost.
  19. Ben: That gives me a practical next step. I understand that receipt of the documents is not confirmation of coverage or the final settlement amount.
  20. Mina: Exactly. Our acknowledgment will confirm receipt, while the review status remains separate. Keep the claim reference on future correspondence so the new information can be connected to the correct file and evaluated in context.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Request the event date and supporting documents

Fictional claims intake: an initial report lacks an event date and supporting documents; no coverage or payment outcome is known.

  1. Claims intake specialist: Could you provide the event date and available supporting documents for this claim through our approved channel?
  2. Broker claims coordinator: The client has not confirmed the date yet. Should I estimate it from the notification email?
  3. Claims intake specialist: No. The notification date may differ from the event date; please identify the uncertainty explicitly.
  4. Broker claims coordinator: I'll ask the client for their verified account and explain which documents are currently available.
  5. Claims intake specialist: Thank you. That helps the adjuster assess the report without filling factual gaps by assumption.
  6. Broker claims coordinator: Does receiving the documents mean the insurer accepts the proof of loss and will pay?
  7. Claims intake specialist: No. Intake and review are separate from a coverage or payment decision.
  8. Broker claims coordinator: Should we tell the client any reserve amount as though it were an offered settlement?
  9. Claims intake specialist: No. A reserve isn't an offer, and case communications must follow the authorized process.
  10. Broker claims coordinator: I'll make the specific information request and confirm realistic availability without promising a claim outcome.
A document list needs a realistic response point

Separate fictional claim-coordination case: an adjuster requests an invoice and photographs; the broker has the photographs but must retrieve the invoice.

  1. Adjuster liaison: Could you provide the invoice and existing photographs so the adjuster can continue the claim review?
  2. Broker claims handler: The photographs are available, but invoice retrieval depends on the client's accounts team.
  3. Adjuster liaison: Please send the available material through the approved channel and identify the invoice as outstanding.
  4. Broker claims handler: Would you prefer that I wait until everything is available before acknowledging the request?
  5. Adjuster liaison: No. A clear partial response helps us understand progress and what still needs follow-up.
  6. Broker claims handler: I can ask accounts for an achievable retrieval time, but I can't promise delivery today.
  7. Adjuster liaison: That's appropriate. Please confirm their response rather than invent a deadline they haven't accepted.
  8. Broker claims handler: Does sending the photographs establish that the claimed amount is already accepted by the adjuster?
  9. Adjuster liaison: No. It supplies evidence for review; it doesn't determine the value or coverage outcome.
  10. Broker claims handler: I'll provide the photographs, flag the missing invoice, and return with a verified retrieval update.
Clarify whose estimate is attached

Separate fictional intake case: a repair estimate is attached without identifying its preparer or assessment date; no amount is approved.

  1. Claims administrator: Could you identify who prepared this repair estimate and when it was assessed?
  2. Broker support officer: The attachment doesn't say. The client forwarded it without the original covering message.
  3. Claims administrator: Please request the source and date so the adjuster can understand the document's basis.
  4. Broker support officer: Can we label it independent assessment because it looks like a professional estimate?
  5. Claims administrator: No. Appearance doesn't establish authorship or independence, and we shouldn't add unsupported credentials.
  6. Broker support officer: I'll seek the original document and preserve the current attachment without editing its missing details.
  7. Claims administrator: Thank you. Please also distinguish this estimate from any proof-of-loss requirements specified for the actual case.
  8. Broker support officer: Understood. Should I tell the client that the estimate sets the final reserve or payment?
  9. Claims administrator: No. Those decisions aren't established by this attachment or this request for information.
  10. Broker support officer: I'll explain the purpose of the request and confirm what the source can provide and when.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Open the file without inventing the loss details
  1. Read turns 1-10, keeping discovery date and unknown event date separate.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; contrast a reserve, a settlement, and payment authorization.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Separate the dates in another intake call

For claim C318, damage was discovered on 12 July. The event date remains unknown. Photographs are received; the repair estimate is missing. No payment decision has been made.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Specialist: "The claim reference is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. C318

C318 is the identifier assigned to the separate claim in this exercise.

2. Caller: "The discovery date is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 12 July

Twelve July is the known discovery date, not a confirmed event date.

3. Specialist: "The missing cost document is the ___."

Answer and explanation

C. repair estimate

The brief states that photographs are received but the repair estimate is missing.

4. Caller: "The event date remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unknown

The case explicitly leaves the event date unknown, so it must not be guessed.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Course: Insurance English
Professional audience: underwriters, claims adjusters, brokers, risk managers, actuarial analysts, policy-service teams, compliance staff, and insurance operations leaders
Scope: All insurers, policies, claims, people, figures, and local procedures are fictional. Lesson 03: Claims Intake and Reserving
Original fictional case: Intake specialist Mina receives notice of alleged water damage under claim C204. The caller, Ben, has provided contact details and photographs, but the event date is unknown and the repair estimate has not been received. The date the damage was discovered is 6 June; that is not necessarily the date the event occurred. An adjuster has not completed the coverage or amount review. Mina will request the known timeline and supporting documents through the approved channel. An internal reserve, if established under the applicable process, is an estimate for claim accounting and management, not a promise of payment to Ben.
Language workshop: Open the file without inventing the loss details
Communication goal: Ask for precise missing claim information and distinguish an estimate from a settlement commitment.
Language guidance: The event date, discovery date, and notification date can differ. Record the known discovery date as such. Do not move it into the event-date field merely because the latter is empty.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We have opened claim C204 and received your photographs. / You discovered the damage on 6 June. / The event date has not yet been established. / Please do not guess a date to fill the form. / Tell us when you first noticed the damage and what records you have. / We still need the repair estimate. / The adjuster needs these details to evaluate the reported loss. / Please use the approved submission route for the documents. / We have your contact details and photographs. / I will check the file before asking you to resend anything. / Include C204 with the supporting material. / Coverage and amount reviews are not complete. / A reserve is not a promise of payment. / No payment is authorized by this intake conversation. / I will record the date as unknown rather than inferred. / We need the known timeline and repair estimate, not an invented event date.
Vocabulary: first notice of loss (FNOL): The initial report of an event or loss to an insurer. / claimant: A person or entity making a claim. / policyholder: The person or organization holding the policy. / claim number: The identifier assigned to a claim file. / date of loss: The date associated with the event causing the claimed loss, as applicable. / discovery date: The date the loss or damage was first noticed. / notification date: The date the insurer or relevant recipient was notified. / claim intake: The initial collection and recording of claim information. / adjuster: A person responsible for investigating or evaluating claims within their role. / supporting documentation: Records relevant to evaluating the claimed event or amount. / repair estimate: A projected cost for specified repair work. / proof of loss: A statement or document describing claimed loss as required by the applicable process. / case reserve: An estimate assigned to an individual claim under the reserving process. / unpaid claim estimate: An estimate of amounts remaining to be paid on relevant claims. / paid loss: Claim amounts already paid on the stated basis. / incurred loss: A loss measure combining paid amounts and relevant estimates on a specified basis. / incurred but not reported (IBNR): An estimate for claims or development not yet reflected in reported information on the stated basis. / loss adjustment expense (LAE): Costs associated with investigating and settling claims under the accounting definition used. / settlement: An agreement or resolution concerning the claim under applicable terms. / payment authorization: Approval for a specified claim payment. / claim chronology: A timeline of reported events and claim-handling steps. / information request: A request specifying what additional material is needed. / secure submission channel: An approved route for transmitting sensitive claim information. / acknowledgment: Confirmation that a report or communication was received.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, keeping discovery date and unknown event date separate. Swap roles for turns 11-20; contrast a reserve, a settlement, and payment authorization. Complete the C318 transfer and verify each answer before rereading the intake exchange.
Partner: Ben: Policyholder / Mina: Claims intake specialist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: For claim C318, damage was discovered on 12 July. The event date remains unknown. Photographs are received; the repair estimate is missing. No payment decision has been made.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: Please make the request list clear. I will provide the known dates and the repair estimate when it is available, without guessing the event date.
Mina: I will send that information request and identify what we already hold. This helps you respond to the actual gaps rather than repeat work or infer that every earlier submission has been lost.
Ben: That gives me a practical next step. I understand that receipt of the documents is not confirmation of coverage or the final settlement amount.
Mina: Exactly. Our acknowledgment will confirm receipt, while the review status remains separate. Keep the claim reference on future correspondence so the new information can be connected to the correct file and evaluated in context.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Vocabulary: first notice of loss (FNOL): The initial report of an event or loss to an insurer. / claimant: A person or entity making a claim. / policyholder: The person or organization holding the policy. / claim number: The identifier assigned to a claim file. / date of loss: The date associated with the event causing the claimed loss, as applicable. / discovery date: The date the loss or damage was first noticed. / notification date: The date the insurer or relevant recipient was notified. / claim intake: The initial collection and recording of claim information. / adjuster: A person responsible for investigating or evaluating claims within their role. / supporting documentation: Records relevant to evaluating the claimed event or amount. / repair estimate: A projected cost for specified repair work. / proof of loss: A statement or document describing claimed loss as required by the applicable process. / case reserve: An estimate assigned to an individual claim under the reserving process. / unpaid claim estimate: An estimate of amounts remaining to be paid on relevant claims. / paid loss: Claim amounts already paid on the stated basis. / incurred loss: A loss measure combining paid amounts and relevant estimates on a specified basis. / incurred but not reported (IBNR): An estimate for claims or development not yet reflected in reported information on the stated basis. / loss adjustment expense (LAE): Costs associated with investigating and settling claims under the accounting definition used. / settlement: An agreement or resolution concerning the claim under applicable terms. / payment authorization: Approval for a specified claim payment. / claim chronology: A timeline of reported events and claim-handling steps. / information request: A request specifying what additional material is needed. / secure submission channel: An approved route for transmitting sensitive claim information. / acknowledgment: Confirmation that a report or communication was received.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, keeping discovery date and unknown event date separate. Swap roles for turns 11-20; contrast a reserve, a settlement, and payment authorization. Complete the C318 transfer and verify each answer before rereading the intake exchange.
Partner: Ben: Policyholder / Mina: Claims intake specialist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: For claim C318, damage was discovered on 12 July. The event date remains unknown. Photographs are received; the repair estimate is missing. No payment decision has been made.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: Please make the request list clear. I will provide the known dates and the repair estimate when it is available, without guessing the event date.
Mina: I will send that information request and identify what we already hold. This helps you respond to the actual gaps rather than repeat work or infer that every earlier submission has been lost.
Ben: That gives me a practical next step. I understand that receipt of the documents is not confirmation of coverage or the final settlement amount.
Mina: Exactly. Our acknowledgment will confirm receipt, while the review status remains separate. Keep the claim reference on future correspondence so the new information can be connected to the correct file and evaluated in context.
END REFERENCE
04Coverage Disputes and DenialsExplain an open review without implying a denial+

Your goal

Respond to pressure for certainty with an accurate status, specific outstanding issue, and useful follow-up.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Explain an open review without implying a denial

Policyholder Miles reports equipment damage under claim K91 and asks whether the loss is definitely covered. Claims representative Sana confirms that the review is open. The cause report is outstanding, and the applicable endorsement must be evaluated against the facts. No coverage determination, denial, or payment commitment has been made. Sana will provide a status update Friday at 16:00, whether or not the review is complete. Miles interprets the request for more information as a rejection. Sana must correct that impression without promising acceptance or implying that a pending review suspends any applicable rights or deadlines.

Miles: Policyholder / Sana: Claims representative

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the claim's current status?

Answer and explanation

C. Coverage review remains open.

The brief states that review is open and no determination, denial, or payment commitment has been made.

2. Which information is outstanding?

Answer and explanation

B. The cause report

The cause report is specifically identified as outstanding in the current review.

3. What is promised for Friday at 16:00?

Answer and explanation

A. A status update even if review remains incomplete

Sana commits to reporting status, not to a particular decision or completed review.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Pending is neither accepted nor denied. A request for relevant information is not itself a denial, and a promised update is not a promised outcome. Actual notices must comply with applicable requirements.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
coverage determination
A decision about whether and how policy coverage applies to a claim.issue a coverage determination
coverage review
Evaluation of the policy and facts relevant to a claim's coverage.complete a coverage review
coverage position
The insurer's stated interpretation or decision concerning coverage.explain the coverage position
denial
A decision refusing a claim or specified part of it on a stated basis.communicate a denial
partial coverage
Coverage applying to some but not all claimed matters under the decision.explain partial coverage
reservation of rights
A notice preserving specified coverage rights while a matter is handled, subject to applicable law.review a reservation of rights
cause report
A report examining how an event or damage occurred.obtain the cause report
causation
The relationship between an event and the claimed loss.assess causation
factual basis
The established information supporting a conclusion.explain the factual basis
policy provision
A specific term or clause in the policy.cite the policy provision
applicable endorsement
The amendment relevant to the policy, period, and issue under review.evaluate the applicable endorsement
unresolved issue
A question not yet answered sufficiently for the relevant decision.identify the unresolved issue
review owner
The person responsible for coordinating the relevant evaluation.identify the review owner
status update
A communication reporting the current stage and known developments.provide a status update
decision notice
A communication conveying a specified decision.distinguish the decision notice
review route
The applicable process for requesting further examination of a decision.explain the review route
appeal
A request for reconsideration through an available formal process.identify the applicable appeal process
complaint
An expression of dissatisfaction requiring handling under the relevant process.record a complaint
rights and remedies
Available entitlements and means of pursuing them under the applicable framework.explain relevant rights and remedies
notice requirement
A rule or term governing a required communication.check the notice requirement
time limit
A period within which a specified action must be taken.verify the applicable time limit
without prejudice
A context-dependent legal expression whose effect requires applicable legal assessment.seek advice on without-prejudice wording
settlement authority
The defined power to approve a claim resolution or payment arrangement.confirm settlement authority
written explanation
A documented account of the reasons and supporting basis for a position.provide a written explanation

Do not assume an open review pauses a policyholder deadline or removes a complaint or review right. Such questions require the applicable policy, jurisdiction, and authorized advice; this dialogue creates no legal extension.

C Notice the language

Acknowledge the need without borrowing certainty

A policyholder may need a definite answer to make practical decisions. Acknowledge that pressure, explain the actual unresolved issue, and provide a reliable communication step. Neither reassurance nor defensive language should change the claim status.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the practical need
I understand that you need clarity for your next decisions.
State the actual status
The coverage review remains open.
Correct the inference
The information request is not a denial.
Avoid the opposite promise
It is not confirmation of coverage either.
Name the missing item
We are waiting for the cause report.
Explain the other task
The endorsement must be evaluated against the facts.
Keep the decision separate
No coverage determination has been made.
Avoid payment language
I cannot promise payment from the current status.
Offer a reliable contact
I will update you Friday at 16:00.
Limit the time commitment
That is an update time, not a guaranteed decision date.
Keep communication active
I will contact you even if the review is incomplete.
Respect other rights
Do not assume this review changes any applicable deadline.
Clarify the request
The missing report is needed to assess the reported cause.
Use plain status words
Open review means the decision has not yet been reached.
Explain an eventual position
A decision should be communicated through the applicable process with its basis.
Close the call
We will separate the outstanding evidence, review status, and next contact.
6 language and precision notes
Definitely covered
Claims a determination that has not been made here.
Not a denial
Corrects rejection without implying acceptance.
Waiting for
Identifies a specific dependency, not an excuse to stop communicating.
Even if
Keeps the update commitment valid despite an incomplete review.
Any applicable deadline
Avoids inventing a universal time limit or extension.
Decision versus status
A report about progress is not necessarily a coverage ruling.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response best matches the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. The review is open; the cause report is outstanding; no coverage decision has been made.

The accurate response identifies the actual stage and missing evidence without inventing acceptance, rejection, or payment.

2. What should Sana do if review is incomplete on Friday?

Answer and explanation

D. Provide the promised status update and explain what remains open.

The communication commitment remains in force even when the substantive review is unfinished.

3. Which statement about deadlines is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

D. The open review automatically pauses every applicable deadline.

No supplied policy or legal basis establishes that pending review suspends any deadline.

4. How should an eventual decision be described?

Answer and explanation

C. Through the applicable process with its actual basis and relevant next steps

A decision needs its actual factual and policy basis, not a shorthand reference, comparison, or unfinished estimate.

  2. Sana
    The 1 remains open. No coverage determination or denial has been made. I understand that you need clarity, but I should not describe an unfinished decision as either accepted or rejected.
  3. Miles
    Your email says further information required. Please name the missing document so I can follow up.
  4. Sana
    The 2 is outstanding. The applicable endorsement also needs to be evaluated against the facts. Those are the current review issues, rather than a completed conclusion that your loss is outside coverage.
  5. Miles
    Would providing that report guarantee acceptance? I need to decide what to do about the equipment.
  6. Sana
    Receiving it would inform the 3, not guarantee a particular outcome. We still need to assess what it establishes alongside the applicable wording. I cannot turn a document request into a promise of acceptance.
  7. Miles
    Please confirm directly whether anything has been denied. Is this request a decision notice?

    No 4 has been made here. The information request is not one. At the same time, the absence of a denial is not confirmation that the claim is covered.
  9. Miles
    That is clearer. Can you identify who will keep me informed rather than leaving me to call repeatedly for the same status?

    I will coordinate the 5 Friday at sixteen hundred. I will contact you even if the review is incomplete, explain the actual position, and identify what remains unresolved rather than simply repeat a generic message.

    Should I treat Friday as the date when the claim will be decided? That would at least give me a fixed planning point.
  12. Sana

  13. Miles
    Please explain how the provision fits the facts, rather than giving me an endorsement number alone.
  14. Sana
    The eventual explanation must identify the relevant 7 and how it relates to the established facts. I will not guess that relationship before the review or substitute a broad label for the applicable wording.
  15. Miles
    Does an open review mean I can ignore any time limits elsewhere in the policy while I wait for your answer?
  16. Sana
    Do not assume that. An applicable 8 needs to be checked under the policy and relevant requirements. This conversation creates no extension and does not remove any rights or obligations that may apply.
  17. Miles
    If I disagree with the eventual decision, will the available review routes be explained?
  18. Sana
    The applicable 9 and complaint options should be communicated as required for the actual decision and jurisdiction. We should not invent a universal procedure or suggest that this current request takes those options away.
  19. Miles
    For now, I understand that you need the cause report, the endorsement review is ongoing, and I will receive a status call Friday.
  20. Sana
    Correct. No 10 has been made or promised through this exchange. I will keep the evidence request, review status, and next contact clear so you are not left to infer a decision from silence.

Answers and explanations
  1. coverage review Turn 2. Coverage review is the ongoing evaluation; its open status does not establish acceptance or denial.
  2. cause report Turn 4. The cause report is the specified outstanding evidence about how the damage occurred; naming it makes the dependency clear.
  3. factual basis Turn 6. The factual basis supports evaluation; supplying one document does not predetermine the resulting coverage decision.
  4. denial Turn 8. Denial is a decision refusing all or part of a claim; none has been made in the supplied case.
  5. status update Turn 10. A status update reports progress and outstanding matters; it is not automatically a decision or payment promise.
  6. coverage determination Turn 12. A coverage determination decides application of the policy; the Friday communication commitment does not guarantee that decision.
  7. policy provision Turn 14. A policy provision supplies the actual wording basis; an unexplained reference to a clause is not a clear coverage explanation.
  8. time limit Turn 16. A time limit remains governed by its actual requirements; this pending review does not establish an automatic suspension or extension.
  9. review route Turn 18. The review route depends on the actual policy and jurisdiction; the dialogue does not invent a universal appeal process or deadline.
  10. payment authorization Turn 20. Payment authorization permits a specified payment; no such commitment follows from the pending review or promised update.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Review underway is not confirmed coverage

Fictional claims discussion: a reported loss remains under review; no coverage position has been finalized.

  1. Broker claims advocate: The client wants to know whether this loss is definitely covered before arranging their next steps.
  2. Claims liaison: The review is still underway, so we don't have a finalized coverage position to communicate.
  3. Broker claims advocate: The claim number was issued. Doesn't that indicate the insurer has accepted the claim?
  4. Claims liaison: It confirms a case record exists, not that coverage or payment has been accepted.
  5. Broker claims advocate: Then I should explain the confirmed registration and keep the coverage conclusion explicitly pending.
  6. Claims liaison: Yes. We can request a status update from the authorized claims team without predicting their decision.
  7. Broker claims advocate: Would pending mean the client should assume a denial and begin describing the matter as an appeal?
  8. Claims liaison: No. The supplied status establishes neither a denial nor the applicable next-stage process.
  9. Broker claims advocate: I'll avoid both reassurance and a negative prediction while asking what information remains under review.
  10. Claims liaison: Good. The client deserves a precise status, not certainty that the current evidence doesn't support.
A reservation letter is not necessarily a final denial

Separate fictional case: a file contains a reservation-of-rights letter, but the team's summary calls the claim denied; the full case position needs authorized review.

  1. Claims coordination manager: The summary says denied, but the document is titled reservation of rights. Are those the same status?
  2. Coverage review liaison: We shouldn't treat them as interchangeable. The actual letter and case position need qualified review.
  3. Claims coordination manager: Could the summary simply say a letter was issued while we verify its specific implications?
  4. Coverage review liaison: Yes. Identify the document accurately without assigning a final denial that hasn't been established here.
  5. Claims coordination manager: The broker asks whether an appeal deadline has started. Can we answer from the title alone?
  6. Coverage review liaison: No. Applicable processes and timing require the actual documents, facts, and authorized guidance.
  7. Claims coordination manager: I'll request clarification of the current coverage position and avoid giving an invented procedural deadline.
  8. Coverage review liaison: Good. Keep the letter's existence separate from your interpretation of what it means.
  9. Claims coordination manager: I'll correct the summary and explain that the final position isn't confirmed by our current information.
  10. Coverage review liaison: That preserves the uncertainty and directs the substantive coverage question to the appropriate reviewer.
A similar paid claim does not settle this loss

Separate fictional broker meeting: a client cites a previous paid claim, but the present loss's facts and applicable wording are still being assessed.

  1. Broker account manager: The client says a similar claim was paid last year, so this one must be covered.
  2. Claims review specialist: That history doesn't establish this case's outcome. We still need its facts and applicable policy wording.
  3. Broker account manager: Can I acknowledge the earlier payment without suggesting it guarantees the same coverage position now?
  4. Claims review specialist: Yes. Explain that the current review remains open and similarity hasn't been established in all relevant respects.
  5. Broker account manager: Would the prior payment at least prove there can be no exclusion affecting this claim?
  6. Claims review specialist: No. We cannot infer that from the payment alone or replace review of the current documents.
  7. Broker account manager: I'll ask the client for the prior reference as potentially relevant context, not decisive proof.
  8. Claims review specialist: Good. Keep any authorized comparison separate from your initial status communication to the client.
  9. Broker account manager: I'll say the earlier outcome is noted, while this loss's coverage position remains under assessment.
  10. Claims review specialist: That responds to the client's concern without promising payment or predicting a denial before review.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Course: Insurance English
Professional audience: underwriters, claims adjusters, brokers, risk managers, actuarial analysts, policy-service teams, compliance staff, and insurance operations leaders
Scope: All insurers, policies, claims, people, figures, and local procedures are fictional. Lesson 04: Coverage Disputes and Denials
Original fictional case: Policyholder Miles reports equipment damage under claim K91 and asks whether the loss is definitely covered. Claims representative Sana confirms that the review is open. The cause report is outstanding, and the applicable endorsement must be evaluated against the facts. No coverage determination, denial, or payment commitment has been made. Sana will provide a status update Friday at 16:00, whether or not the review is complete. Miles interprets the request for more information as a rejection. Sana must correct that impression without promising acceptance or implying that a pending review suspends any applicable rights or deadlines.
Language workshop: Explain an open review without implying a denial
Communication goal: Respond to pressure for certainty with an accurate status, specific outstanding issue, and useful follow-up.
Language guidance: Pending is neither accepted nor denied. A request for relevant information is not itself a denial, and a promised update is not a promised outcome. Actual notices must comply with applicable requirements.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that you need clarity for your next decisions. / The coverage review remains open. / The information request is not a denial. / It is not confirmation of coverage either. / We are waiting for the cause report. / The endorsement must be evaluated against the facts. / No coverage determination has been made. / I cannot promise payment from the current status. / I will update you Friday at 16:00. / That is an update time, not a guaranteed decision date. / I will contact you even if the review is incomplete. / Do not assume this review changes any applicable deadline. / The missing report is needed to assess the reported cause. / Open review means the decision has not yet been reached. / A decision should be communicated through the applicable process with its basis. / We will separate the outstanding evidence, review status, and next contact.
Vocabulary: coverage determination: A decision about whether and how policy coverage applies to a claim. / coverage review: Evaluation of the policy and facts relevant to a claim's coverage. / coverage position: The insurer's stated interpretation or decision concerning coverage. / denial: A decision refusing a claim or specified part of it on a stated basis. / partial coverage: Coverage applying to some but not all claimed matters under the decision. / reservation of rights: A notice preserving specified coverage rights while a matter is handled, subject to applicable law. / cause report: A report examining how an event or damage occurred. / causation: The relationship between an event and the claimed loss. / factual basis: The established information supporting a conclusion. / policy provision: A specific term or clause in the policy. / applicable endorsement: The amendment relevant to the policy, period, and issue under review. / unresolved issue: A question not yet answered sufficiently for the relevant decision. / review owner: The person responsible for coordinating the relevant evaluation. / status update: A communication reporting the current stage and known developments. / decision notice: A communication conveying a specified decision. / review route: The applicable process for requesting further examination of a decision. / appeal: A request for reconsideration through an available formal process. / complaint: An expression of dissatisfaction requiring handling under the relevant process. / rights and remedies: Available entitlements and means of pursuing them under the applicable framework. / notice requirement: A rule or term governing a required communication. / time limit: A period within which a specified action must be taken. / without prejudice: A context-dependent legal expression whose effect requires applicable legal assessment. / settlement authority: The defined power to approve a claim resolution or payment arrangement. / written explanation: A documented account of the reasons and supporting basis for a position.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, acknowledging the practical problem before giving the review status. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the Friday update from a decision deadline. Complete the M22 transfer and check the missing report, contact, and update time.
Partner: Miles: Policyholder / Sana: Claims representative
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Claim M22 awaits an inspection report. No coverage determination has been made. Rehan will provide a status update Tuesday at 14:00, even if the review remains incomplete.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Miles: If I disagree with the eventual decision, will the available review routes be explained?
Sana: The applicable review route and complaint options should be communicated as required for the actual decision and jurisdiction. We should not invent a universal procedure or suggest that this current request takes those options away.
Miles: For now, I understand that you need the cause report, the endorsement review is ongoing, and I will receive a status call Friday.
Sana: Correct. No payment authorization has been made or promised through this exchange. I will keep the evidence request, review status, and next contact clear so you are not left to infer a decision from silence.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Broker and Client Renewal MeetingsLower premium does not mean identical protection+

Your goal

Explain a renewal trade-off using a bounded deductible calculation and a complete comparison request.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Lower premium does not mean identical protection

Broker Naomi compares two annual renewal quotes for client Victor. Quote A has a $10,000 premium and a $1,000 per-loss deductible; quote B has a $9,000 premium and a $5,000 per-loss deductible. Their full wording has not been compared, and neither quote is bound. For one arithmetic illustration only, assume a fully covered $8,000 loss, sufficient applicable limits, no coinsurance, no other adjustments, and a deductible subtracted once from the loss. A would leave $1,000 of that loss with Victor; B would leave $5,000. The illustration is not a prediction of future claims or a coverage decision.

Victor: Business client / Naomi: Insurance broker

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How much lower is B's annual premium?

Answer and explanation

A. $1,000

Ten thousand minus nine thousand gives a one-thousand-dollar annual premium difference.

2. How much higher is B's per-loss deductible?

Answer and explanation

C. $4,000

Five thousand minus one thousand gives a four-thousand-dollar increase in the stated deductible.

3. What is not yet established?

Answer and explanation

C. That the full coverage wording is otherwise identical

The full wording has not been compared, so equivalent coverage cannot be asserted.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

In the $8,000 illustration, A pays $7,000 and B pays $3,000, with Victor bearing $1,000 and $5,000 respectively. These results depend on all stated assumptions and do not decide actual claim coverage.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
renewal
Continuation or replacement of insurance for a further policy period.review the renewal
renewal quote
Proposed terms for the next policy period.compare renewal quotes
annual premium
The stated insurance price for a one-year period on the specified basis.compare the annual premium
per-loss deductible
The deductible applied to each loss under the stated terms.explain the per-loss deductible
premium saving
A reduction in premium relative to a specified comparison.quantify the premium saving
retained loss
The part of a loss borne by the insured under the arrangement.compare retained loss
out-of-pocket amount
Money the client bears directly on the defined basis.estimate the out-of-pocket amount
coverage comparison
Review of differences in the protection and terms offered.prepare a coverage comparison
like-for-like basis
A comparison holding relevant terms and assumptions consistent.compare on a like-for-like basis
coinsurance
A cost-sharing or insurance-to-value provision whose meaning depends on the policy type.review the coinsurance provision
valuation basis
The method used to determine the value of a covered loss.compare the valuation basis
replacement cost
A valuation basis concerning replacement under the applicable terms.review replacement-cost terms
actual cash value
A valuation measure defined by applicable policy terms and law, often reflecting depreciation.explain actual cash value
depreciation
A reduction in value attributed to age, wear, or other relevant factors.account for depreciation
sublimit comparison
Review of differences in specified lower coverage limits.complete a sublimit comparison
coverage gap
An identified absence or interruption of relevant insurance protection.identify a coverage gap
renewal condition
A stated requirement affecting the proposed renewal.confirm a renewal condition
quote validity
The period and conditions during which proposed terms remain available.check quote validity
binding instruction
A request to place coverage under the applicable authority and process.confirm binding instructions
binding confirmation
Communication confirming placed coverage according to its actual terms.obtain binding confirmation
effective period
The dates during which the relevant terms apply.verify the effective period
illustrative loss
A hypothetical loss used to demonstrate a calculation.label the illustrative loss
total-cost comparison
Comparison including specified premium and loss or other cost components.state the total-cost comparison basis
risk tolerance
Willingness and capacity to bear uncertainty or loss under a defined context.discuss risk tolerance

B saves $1,000 in annual premium but retains $4,000 more in this one-loss illustration. Combining premium and retained loss gives $11,000 for A and $14,000 for B in that illustration only, not an expected annual-cost forecast.

C Notice the language

Explain the trade-off before asking for a choice

A client may focus on the most visible price. Acknowledge the premium saving, then quantify the changed deductible under explicit assumptions. Do not hide the trade-off or present a simplified example as proof of which option will be best for the client.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the saving
B's annual premium is $1,000 lower.
Name the changed term
B's per-loss deductible is $4,000 higher.
Reject false equivalence
These are not identical terms for less money.
Keep the review open
We have not yet compared the complete wording.
Set the assumptions
Assume one fully covered $8,000 loss with no other adjustments.
Calculate the first share
Under A, you would retain $1,000 of that loss.
Calculate the second share
Under B, you would retain $5,000 of that loss.
State the insurer amount
The illustrated insurer payments are $7,000 and $3,000.
Limit the example
This is arithmetic practice, not a prediction or coverage decision.
Include the full basis
Premium plus retained loss totals $11,000 under A in this example.
Contrast the example
The same illustrated total is $14,000 under B.
Avoid universal advice
One hypothetical loss does not establish the best option for every future year.
Check other differences
We still need the exclusions, endorsements, limits, and valuation terms.
Preserve quote status
Neither quote is currently bound.
Ask for a documented decision
Confirm the actual terms before giving binding instructions.
Close the comparison
We will show the premium saving and the changed exposure together.
6 language and precision notes
Lower premium
Describes price, not automatically the scope or value of protection.
Per loss
Matters because application can differ from annual or aggregate arrangements.
Would under these assumptions
Keeps the calculation conditional.
Fully covered
A supplied illustration assumption, not a conclusion about a real claim.
Neither bound
Describes placement status, not merely the existence of a quote.
Expected versus illustrative
A single example does not estimate the distribution of future outcomes.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. In the illustration, what would A's insurer payment be?

Answer and explanation

B. $7,000

The assumed fully covered loss of eight thousand minus A's one-thousand deductible gives seven thousand.

2. In the illustration, what would B's insurer payment be?

Answer and explanation

A. $3,000

Eight thousand minus B's five-thousand deductible gives three thousand under the stated assumptions.

3. Which premium-plus-retained-loss comparison is correct for this example?

Answer and explanation

D. A: $11,000; B: $14,000

A combines ten thousand premium and one thousand retained loss; B combines nine thousand and five thousand.

4. Which conclusion is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

D. B provides identical coverage and always costs less overall.

The complete wording is unreviewed, and total cost depends on actual losses and terms, not premium alone.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

Lower premium does not mean identical protection

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Victor: Business client / Naomi: Insurance broker

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • valuation basis
  • premium saving
  • like-for-like basis
  • retained loss
  • total-cost comparison
  • effective period
  • annual premium
  • Coinsurance
  • illustrative loss
  • binding confirmation
  1. Victor
    Quote B is a thousand dollars cheaper annually. Is this simply the same protection for less money, or is there a trade-off?
  2. Naomi
    The 1 is lower, but B's deductible is five thousand rather than one thousand dollars. We also have not compared the full wording, so we cannot claim equivalent protection.
  3. Victor
    The deductible difference is four thousand dollars per loss. Could you show how that would matter using the eight-thousand-dollar example?
  4. Naomi
    Yes, as an 2 only. Assume it is fully covered, limits are sufficient, no coinsurance or other adjustment applies, and the deductible is subtracted once. Those assumptions are essential to the calculation.
  5. Victor
    Under A, subtracting one thousand from eight thousand gives a seven-thousand-dollar insurer payment. My share of that loss would be one thousand.
  6. Naomi
    Correct. That is your 3 in this example. Under B, the five-thousand-dollar deductible leaves a three-thousand-dollar insurer payment and five thousand for you to bear.
  7. Victor
    So B's premium saving is one thousand, but I retain four thousand more if this particular hypothetical loss occurs. Those are different comparisons.
  8. Naomi
    Exactly. Show the 4 and deductible effect side by side. Do not describe the saving as a guaranteed reduction in every cost you could face during the policy year.
  9. Victor
    If I include annual premium and my share of this one loss, I get eleven thousand for A and fourteen thousand for B.
  10. Naomi
    That is the correct 5 on this narrow basis. It is not an expected annual-cost forecast. Different losses, no losses, or other policy terms could produce different outcomes.
  11. Victor
    Could another term affect the calculation even if the main limit shown on both quotes looks the same? We have only compared the headline figures.
  12. Naomi
    Yes. The 6, exclusions, endorsements, sublimits, and other applicable provisions need review. A matching headline limit does not prove that the full coverage or settlement basis is identical.
  13. Victor
    One quote also mentions coinsurance. We have left that out of the arithmetic, so I still need its actual effect explained.
  14. Naomi
    Correct. 7 requires the relevant wording and policy context. We should not transfer a definition from another product or assume the simplified illustration settles how that provision applies.
  15. Victor
    Please keep the example separate from your recommendation. Our decision also depends on which losses we can afford to retain.
  16. Naomi
    It is not. A 8 requires the complete terms, and a decision also needs your actual needs and capacity to bear loss. One hypothetical event cannot decide every possible future year.
  17. Victor
    Have either of these terms already been placed? I have not instructed you to proceed with a particular quote.
  18. Naomi
    Neither has 9. We must keep proposed terms separate from actual placement. A discussion of the lower premium or our calculation does not by itself confirm coverage under either quote.
  19. Victor
    Please finish the wording comparison and show the premium and deductible differences clearly. Then we can make a documented decision about the proposal.
  20. Naomi
    Agreed. We will confirm the 10 and other relevant conditions before acting on binding instructions. The comparison should make the trade-offs visible, not hide them behind the lower annual price.

Answers and explanations
  1. annual premium Turn 2. Annual premium describes the stated yearly insurance price; a lower figure does not establish identical protection.
  2. illustrative loss Turn 4. An illustrative loss is hypothetical and demonstrates arithmetic under explicit assumptions rather than deciding actual coverage.
  3. retained loss Turn 6. Retained loss is the loss amount borne by the client; A retains one thousand under the supplied assumptions.
  4. premium saving Turn 8. Premium saving compares the insurance price, while the higher retained loss is a separate effect of the changed deductible.
  5. total-cost comparison Turn 10. The total-cost comparison includes premium and retained loss for this single hypothetical case, not every possible annual outcome.
  6. valuation basis Turn 12. Valuation basis affects how loss value is determined under the policy; matching headline limits do not establish identical payment terms.
  7. Coinsurance Turn 14. Coinsurance has policy-specific cost-sharing or insurance-to-value meanings; this illustration expressly excludes it rather than interpreting an actual provision.
  8. like-for-like basis Turn 16. A like-for-like basis aligns relevant terms and assumptions before comparison; the full quote wording has not yet been aligned here.
  9. binding confirmation Turn 18. Binding confirmation records actual placed coverage; receiving or discussing a quote does not establish that it has been bound.
  10. effective period Turn 20. The effective period identifies when the actual terms apply; it must be checked with the proposal before placement instructions are acted on.
Read the complete conversation

  2. Naomi: The annual premium is lower, but B's deductible is five thousand rather than one thousand dollars. We also have not compared the full wording, so we cannot claim equivalent protection.
