Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Lower premium, higher deductible

Fictional renewal comparison: A has a premium of 900 units and deductible of 200; B has a premium of 800 and deductible of 500. Other terms remain unreviewed.

  1. Client insurance manager: Quote B costs one hundred less. Is it the same protection at a lower price?
  2. Broker renewal specialist: Its deductible is five hundred rather than two hundred, and we haven't compared all other terms.
  3. Client insurance manager: So the lower premium comes with a higher stated deductible, not a verified equivalent policy.
  4. Broker renewal specialist: Correct. We should compare coverage wording, limits, exclusions, and deductible terms on the same basis.
  5. Client insurance manager: Can we calculate my exact cost for any future claim from those two numbers alone?
  6. Broker renewal specialist: No. The applicable terms and facts matter; this comparison doesn't determine a particular claim payment.
  7. Client insurance manager: Then I'll present the premium saving alongside the increased deductible instead of declaring B better.
  8. Broker renewal specialist: Good. We also need the same policy period and exposure assumptions in the comparison.
  9. Client insurance manager: I'll request the complete quotations and take the tradeoff to our authorized decision maker.
  10. Broker renewal specialist: I'll identify the verified differences and unresolved terms without recommending coverage from price alone.
A shorter quote period distorts the saving

Separate fictional renewal case: one quotation covers twelve months and another nine months; the scope and other terms have not been matched.

  1. Finance coordinator: The second renewal quote is cheaper. Can we report an annual premium saving to management?
  2. Broker placement adviser: The second quote covers nine months, while the first covers twelve. They aren't annual equivalents.
  3. Finance coordinator: Could I multiply the nine-month price to create a guaranteed twelve-month quotation for comparison?
  4. Broker placement adviser: That would be an illustration, not a supplier quote, and might ignore different terms or pricing conditions.
  5. Finance coordinator: Then we should request quotations for the same period before presenting a confirmed saving.
  6. Broker placement adviser: Yes. Use matching exposures, limits, deductibles, and relevant wording as well as time periods.
  7. Finance coordinator: The shorter term may suit our planned review date, but that's a different tradeoff from lower annual cost.
  8. Broker placement adviser: Exactly. State that scheduling preference explicitly without calling the protection or price equivalent.
  9. Finance coordinator: I'll ask for the matched information and keep the nine-month option clearly labeled in the renewal pack.
  10. Broker placement adviser: Good. That lets the decision maker compare real differences rather than a misleading headline discount.
An additional service is not automatically broader coverage

Separate fictional renewal comparison: quote A includes a risk-review visit; quote B does not. Policy wording and the visit's scope remain unreviewed.

  1. Client risk coordinator: Quote A includes a risk-review visit. Does that mean its insurance coverage is broader than B's?
  2. Broker relationship manager: Not necessarily. A service feature and the policy's coverage terms are different comparison categories.
  3. Client risk coordinator: Then we should compare the visit separately from limits, exclusions, premium, and deductible details.
  4. Broker relationship manager: Yes. We also need the visit's actual scope before describing its value to your team.
  5. Client risk coordinator: Someone mentioned reinsurance support in the marketing material. Does that guarantee a better claim outcome?
  6. Broker relationship manager: We shouldn't make that inference. Marketing references don't replace the applicable policy and claims review.
  7. Client risk coordinator: I'll list the included service as a verified feature, with its scope still awaiting clarification.
  8. Broker relationship manager: Good. Ask for comparable policy documents before treating either option as more comprehensive protection.
  9. Client risk coordinator: Our decision discussion will separate service convenience from the financial and coverage terms needing review.
  10. Broker relationship manager: That gives the client a fair renewal comparison without an unsupported recommendation based on one feature.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Lower premium does not mean identical protection
  1. Read turns 1-10 with the $8,000 loss assumption and both retained amounts stated clearly.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; separate the arithmetic example from a binding instruction.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Compare a second pair of deductibles

For one fully covered $6,000 loss, assume sufficient limits and no other adjustments. Quote C has a $500 per-loss deductible; D has a $2,000 deductible. Deductibles are subtracted once.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Broker: "Under C, the client retains ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. 500

C's five-hundred-dollar deductible is the retained amount under the supplied assumptions.

2. Client: "Under C, the insurer pays ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. 5,500

Six thousand minus five hundred gives five thousand five hundred dollars.

3. Broker: "Under D, the client retains ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. 2,000

D's stated two-thousand-dollar deductible is the client's retained amount in this illustration.

4. Client: "Under D, the insurer pays ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. 4,000

Six thousand minus two thousand gives four thousand dollars under the same assumptions.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 05: Broker and Client Renewal Meetings
Original fictional case: Broker Naomi compares two annual renewal quotes for client Victor. Quote A has a $10,000 premium and a $1,000 per-loss deductible; quote B has a $9,000 premium and a $5,000 per-loss deductible. Their full wording has not been compared, and neither quote is bound. For one arithmetic illustration only, assume a fully covered $8,000 loss, sufficient applicable limits, no coinsurance, no other adjustments, and a deductible subtracted once from the loss. A would leave $1,000 of that loss with Victor; B would leave $5,000. The illustration is not a prediction of future claims or a coverage decision.
Language workshop: Lower premium does not mean identical protection
Communication goal: Explain a renewal trade-off using a bounded deductible calculation and a complete comparison request.
Language guidance: In the $8,000 illustration, A pays $7,000 and B pays $3,000, with Victor bearing $1,000 and $5,000 respectively. These results depend on all stated assumptions and do not decide actual claim coverage.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): B's annual premium is $1,000 lower. / B's per-loss deductible is $4,000 higher. / These are not identical terms for less money. / We have not yet compared the complete wording. / Assume one fully covered $8,000 loss with no other adjustments. / Under A, you would retain $1,000 of that loss. / Under B, you would retain $5,000 of that loss. / The illustrated insurer payments are $7,000 and $3,000. / This is arithmetic practice, not a prediction or coverage decision. / Premium plus retained loss totals $11,000 under A in this example. / The same illustrated total is $14,000 under B. / One hypothetical loss does not establish the best option for every future year. / We still need the exclusions, endorsements, limits, and valuation terms. / Neither quote is currently bound. / Confirm the actual terms before giving binding instructions. / We will show the premium saving and the changed exposure together.
Vocabulary: renewal: Continuation or replacement of insurance for a further policy period. / renewal quote: Proposed terms for the next policy period. / annual premium: The stated insurance price for a one-year period on the specified basis. / per-loss deductible: The deductible applied to each loss under the stated terms. / premium saving: A reduction in premium relative to a specified comparison. / retained loss: The part of a loss borne by the insured under the arrangement. / out-of-pocket amount: Money the client bears directly on the defined basis. / coverage comparison: Review of differences in the protection and terms offered. / like-for-like basis: A comparison holding relevant terms and assumptions consistent. / coinsurance: A cost-sharing or insurance-to-value provision whose meaning depends on the policy type. / valuation basis: The method used to determine the value of a covered loss. / replacement cost: A valuation basis concerning replacement under the applicable terms. / actual cash value: A valuation measure defined by applicable policy terms and law, often reflecting depreciation. / depreciation: A reduction in value attributed to age, wear, or other relevant factors. / sublimit comparison: Review of differences in specified lower coverage limits. / coverage gap: An identified absence or interruption of relevant insurance protection. / renewal condition: A stated requirement affecting the proposed renewal. / quote validity: The period and conditions during which proposed terms remain available. / binding instruction: A request to place coverage under the applicable authority and process. / binding confirmation: Communication confirming placed coverage according to its actual terms. / effective period: The dates during which the relevant terms apply. / illustrative loss: A hypothetical loss used to demonstrate a calculation. / total-cost comparison: Comparison including specified premium and loss or other cost components. / risk tolerance: Willingness and capacity to bear uncertainty or loss under a defined context.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 with the $8,000 loss assumption and both retained amounts stated clearly. Swap roles for turns 11-20; separate the arithmetic example from a binding instruction. Complete the C/D transfer and check that each insurer payment plus retained share equals $6,000.
Partner: Victor: Business client / Naomi: Insurance broker
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: For one fully covered $6,000 loss, assume sufficient limits and no other adjustments. Quote C has a $500 per-loss deductible; D has a $2,000 deductible. Deductibles are subtracted once.

Naomi: Neither has binding confirmation. We must keep proposed terms separate from actual placement. A discussion of the lower premium or our calculation does not by itself confirm coverage under either quote.
Victor: Please finish the wording comparison and show the premium and deductible differences clearly. Then we can make a documented decision about the proposal.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Fraud Indicators and SIU ReferralRecord a discrepancy without labeling the customer+

Your goal

Make an objective specialist referral that separates observed records, suspicion, and findings.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Record a discrepancy without labeling the customer

Claims analyst Iris notices that invoice reference P71 from the same repair vendor appears in files Q2 and Q4. The team has not established whether this reflects a duplicate upload, an administrative error, or another explanation. Neither the vendor nor the customer has been found to have acted dishonestly. A draft internal note calls the customer fraudulent. Review lead Hassan will correct that language and refer the documented discrepancy through the approved Special Investigation Unit (SIU) route. Original records must be preserved, access restricted to authorized recipients, and no unsupported allegation added to customer correspondence.

Iris: Claims analyst / Hassan: Review lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has actually been observed?

Answer and explanation

C. Invoice reference P71 appears in Q2 and Q4 for the same vendor.

The supplied evidence establishes a repeated invoice reference, not dishonest intent or an established fraud finding.

2. What remains unknown?

Answer and explanation

C. The explanation for the repeated reference

The case leaves open whether duplication, error, or another explanation accounts for the repeated reference.

3. What should happen to the original records?

Answer and explanation

C. Preserve them and restrict access to authorized recipients.

Preserving originals and limiting access protects traceability and confidentiality during the authorized review.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A repeated invoice reference may justify a closer look, but it does not establish who caused it or why. A referral is an action taken because a concern requires review; it is not a finding of fraud.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
Special Investigation Unit (SIU)
A specialist function reviewing suspected insurance fraud under the applicable process.refer to the SIU
fraud indicator
Information that may warrant examination for possible fraud.document a fraud indicator
suspicion
A concern that has not been established as a finding.distinguish suspicion from proof
allegation
An assertion of wrongdoing that requires appropriate evaluation.avoid an unsupported allegation
verified finding
A conclusion established through the relevant evidential review.record a verified finding
discrepancy
A difference or inconsistency requiring explanation.describe a discrepancy
duplicate record
A second copy or entry that may repeat existing information.identify a duplicate record
invoice reference
An identifier assigned to an invoice.compare the invoice reference
source document
The original or originating record supporting an entry.preserve the source document
administrative error
A mistake in handling or recording information.examine a possible administrative error
referral criteria
The conditions for sending a matter to a specialist review.apply referral criteria
referral summary
A concise account of the concern and supporting evidence.prepare a referral summary
evidence preservation
Retention of relevant material without improper alteration or destruction.maintain evidence preservation
access restriction
A limit on who may view or use information.apply access restrictions
authorized recipient
A person permitted to receive the relevant information.identify authorized recipients
case note
A record of an event, observation, or action in a case file.correct a case note
source attribution
Identification of where information or an assertion originated.include source attribution
chronological record
An account organized by the timing of events or entries.maintain a chronological record
corroboration
Additional evidence supporting a statement or interpretation.seek appropriate corroboration
alternative explanation
Another possible account of the observed information.retain an alternative explanation
investigation status
The current stage of an authorized examination.report investigation status
need-to-know access
Access limited to those requiring the information for their authorized role.maintain need-to-know access
neutral wording
Language describing facts without an unsupported judgment.use neutral wording
review conclusion
The outcome established by the relevant review.distinguish the review conclusion

Correct the accusatory note through the applicable traceable process rather than erase the record of what was written. Share the evidence only through authorized channels. This exercise provides no investigative tactics or fraud-detection threshold.

C Notice the language

Objective language protects the investigation and the person

A forceful label can sound efficient inside a busy team, but it can turn an untested concern into an assumed fact. Describe what is observed, identify alternative explanations that remain open, and let the authorized review establish its findings.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the observation
Invoice P71 appears in both Q2 and Q4 for the same vendor.
Separate observation and conclusion
We have not established the explanation.
Remove an unsupported label
Do not describe the customer as fraudulent.
Keep alternatives open
A duplicate upload or administrative error remains possible.
Preserve the source
Retain the original documents and their references.
Name the next step
Refer the discrepancy through the approved SIU route.
Limit the meaning
Referral is not a finding of fraud.
Use traceable correction
Correct the case note without concealing its history.
Avoid attributing intent
We do not know who caused the repeated entry or why.
Specify the evidence
Include the two file references and the invoice identifier.
Restrict distribution
Send the material only to authorized recipients.
Keep customer communication accurate
Do not insert an unsupported allegation into correspondence.
Report the stage
The concern is awaiting specialist review.
Avoid automatic denial
This referral does not itself determine coverage or payment.
Preserve role boundaries
Let the authorized reviewers establish the findings.
Close objectively
Record the observed discrepancy, open questions, and referral status.
6 language and precision notes
Appears in both
Reports a record comparison without explaining its cause.
May warrant review
Indicates a reason to examine, not an established offense.
Fraudulent
An accusatory conclusion unsupported by these facts.
Remains possible
Preserves an alternative without claiming it is the explanation.
Referred versus proven
An action status differs from an evidential conclusion.
Authorized recipients
Limits access by role rather than curiosity or convenience.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which note is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

A. P71 appears in Q2 and Q4; explanation unconfirmed; referred through the approved SIU route.

The appropriate note records the observation, unresolved explanation, and actual referral action without an unsupported accusation.

2. Which statement confuses referral with a finding?

Answer and explanation

A. SIU referral proves that fraud occurred.

Referral requests specialist examination and does not itself establish wrongdoing.

3. What should the team do with the accusatory draft note?

Answer and explanation

B. Correct it through the traceable process and preserve relevant history.

A traceable correction fixes the wording without hiding the record or treating an allegation as proof.

4. Which explanation is established?

Answer and explanation

B. None of the proposed explanations is yet established.

The brief explicitly leaves the reason unknown, including the possibility of an administrative or upload error.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Record a discrepancy without labeling the customer

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Iris: Claims analyst / Hassan: Review lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • discrepancy
  • alternative explanation
  • investigation status
  • allegation
  • suspicion
  • case note
  • Special Investigation Unit
  • source document
  • review conclusion
  • need-to-know access
  1. Iris
    Invoice P71 appears in both Q2 and Q4 for the same vendor. My draft note calls the customer fraudulent, but that overstates what we know.
  2. Hassan
    Replace the label with the 1 itself. We have identified a repeated reference, not established who caused it or why. The note must distinguish the observed records from an accusation about the person.
  3. Iris
    A duplicate upload or administrative error could explain it. We have not confirmed either explanation, so neither should be presented as the answer.
  4. Hassan
    Keep each 2 open. Neutral language is not a conclusion that nothing is wrong. It is an accurate statement that the explanation still requires authorized review.
  5. Iris
    Should I send the files through our specialist referral route? I want the concern examined without implying that I have completed an investigation.
  6. Hassan
    Use the approved 3 route under the applicable criteria. Include the factual observation and references. Referral does not by itself establish fraud, decide coverage, or authorize a particular claim outcome.
  7. Iris
    I will include the two file identifiers, the invoice reference, and the vendor name from the records. The documents themselves should remain intact.
  8. Hassan
    Preserve each 4 and its context. Do not replace an original with a cleaned-up version that hides how the discrepancy appeared. Reviewers need traceable evidence, not just our description.
  9. Iris
    The accusatory draft is already saved. How do I correct it without leaving the label in place or hiding the change?
  10. Hassan
    Correct the 5 through the applicable process. Preserve the relevant history and make the corrected factual statement clear. We should not leave the accusation unqualified or erase the audit trail.
  11. Iris
    A colleague wants both files sent to the whole claims team. That feels too broad for an unresolved discrepancy.
  12. Hassan
    Maintain 6. Share only with authorized recipients who need the information for their role. An unresolved concern is not a reason to distribute customer information or an unsupported allegation across unrelated teams.
  13. Iris
    I will also check the proposed customer correspondence. It must not repeat the draft label or suggest that specialist referral is proof of wrongdoing.
  14. Hassan
    Correct. Avoid an unsupported 7. Customer communication must follow the authorized process and actual status. Our language task is to preserve that distinction, not invent investigative disclosures or claim decisions.
  15. Iris
    The referral summary should say what we observed, what remains unknown, and what action we took. It should not claim that fraud has been verified.
  16. Hassan
    That separates 8 from a finding. A strong referral can be precise and useful without sounding accusatory. Its value comes from traceable facts and clear questions, not forceful adjectives.
  17. Iris
    When another team asks whether the customer has been cleared or found responsible, I should report only the actual review stage.
  18. Hassan
    Yes. State the 9 available through the authorized route. Do not invent either an adverse finding or an exoneration simply because someone wants a short binary answer before the review is complete.
  19. Iris
    I will correct the note, preserve both records, restrict access, and submit the documented discrepancy. The explanation remains unconfirmed.
  20. Hassan
    Good. Any eventual 10 needs its actual basis. Until then, keep observation, possible explanations, referral action, and decision status separate in every record you prepare.

Answers and explanations
  1. discrepancy Turn 2. Discrepancy describes the repeated reference without asserting a cause, dishonest intent, or established fraud.
  2. alternative explanation Turn 4. An alternative explanation remains a possibility until evaluated; naming one does not establish it as the actual cause.
  3. Special Investigation Unit Turn 6. The Special Investigation Unit is the relevant specialist function; referral requests review rather than declaring the customer guilty.
  4. source document Turn 8. A source document supports the record comparison and must remain available rather than being replaced by a paraphrase or accusation.
  5. case note Turn 10. The case note needs a traceable correction so the unsupported accusation is fixed without concealing the record's history.
  6. need-to-know access Turn 12. Need-to-know access limits sensitive information to authorized roles rather than broad circulation for interest or warning.
  7. allegation Turn 14. An allegation asserts wrongdoing; this case supports a documented concern, not a conclusion to repeat as established fact.
  8. suspicion Turn 16. Suspicion is an unestablished concern; the documented observation may justify review without becoming proof of fraud.
  9. investigation status Turn 18. Investigation status reports the stage of review, not an outcome that has not yet been established.
  10. review conclusion Turn 20. The review conclusion must follow the authorized evidential assessment; it cannot be supplied by the initial analyst's label.

Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An unusual pattern is not established fraud

Fictional internal referral: a claim pattern is unusual, but investigation has not established fraud; sensitive details remain in the approved internal channel.

  1. Claims analyst: The referral draft calls the customer fraudulent because the claim pattern looks unusual.
  2. Investigation supervisor: That conclusion isn't established. Describe the observed pattern as a potential fraud indicator for review.
  3. Claims analyst: Should I explain which records prompted the concern rather than label the customer's character?
  4. Investigation supervisor: Yes. Keep facts, discrepancies, and interpretations distinct when referring to the special investigation unit, or SIU.
  5. Claims analyst: Does referral mean the SIU has already confirmed material misrepresentation in the submission?
  6. Investigation supervisor: No. Referral requests assessment; it doesn't establish intent, misrepresentation, or an investigation outcome.
  7. Claims analyst: I'll remove the accusation and preserve the original supporting records through the approved restricted process.
  8. Investigation supervisor: Good. Don't share sensitive case details in general team discussions or contact the customer outside authorization.
  9. Claims analyst: The revised note will identify the concern and unanswered questions without asserting guilt.
  10. Investigation supervisor: That supports a fair investigation while keeping the language proportionate to the evidence actually available.
Two matching amounts need context

Separate fictional internal review: two claims contain identical estimate amounts; preparers and underlying circumstances have not been compared.

  1. Claims review officer: These two estimates have identical amounts. Is that enough to say the documents were fabricated?
  2. SIU liaison: No. Matching amounts are an observation; fabrication is a conclusion requiring additional evidence and investigation.
  3. Claims review officer: We haven't compared the preparers or underlying circumstances, so several explanations remain open.
  4. SIU liaison: Exactly. Record the similarity accurately and identify the context that hasn't been established.
  5. Claims review officer: Should I describe the match as a possible fraud indicator in the authorized referral?
  6. SIU liaison: You can identify the concern neutrally, following the internal referral process rather than making an accusation.
  7. Claims review officer: Could we alter the case label to confirmed fraud so it receives a faster review?
  8. SIU liaison: No. Urgency doesn't justify a false status or prejudging the people involved.
  9. Claims review officer: I'll preserve the estimates and request review of the matching amounts with their source details.
  10. SIU liaison: Good. A precise observation gives investigators something to assess without substituting suspicion for a finding.
A corrected answer does not establish intent

Separate fictional submission review: a customer corrects an earlier answer about an event; the reason for the difference has not been established.

  1. Claims coordinator: The customer changed an answer. Can the referral say they intentionally misrepresented the event?
  2. Investigation reviewer: The changed answer is documented, but intention hasn't been established from that difference alone.
  3. Claims coordinator: Then I should preserve both versions and state exactly what changed without choosing a motive.
  4. Investigation reviewer: Yes. The authorized investigation can examine the context and any relevant supporting evidence.
  5. Claims coordinator: Would calling it material misrepresentation merely summarize the discrepancy in more professional language?
  6. Investigation reviewer: No. That term carries a substantive conclusion we shouldn't assert without the appropriate basis and review.
  7. Claims coordinator: I'll use a neutral description and identify the unanswered question about why the answer changed.
  8. Investigation reviewer: Good. Keep the referral and any customer contact within the approved process for this case.
  9. Claims coordinator: I'll avoid promising either an adverse finding or exoneration while the facts are still assessed.
  10. Investigation reviewer: That preserves fairness and gives the reviewer a reliable account of what the evidence actually shows.

  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; use the neutral observation rather than the accusatory label.

Answer and explanation

A. R88

R88 is the specific repeated receipt identifier supplied in this case.

2. Reviewer: "It appears in H3 and ___."

Answer and explanation

C. H7

H7 is the second file named in the observed record comparison.

3. Analyst: "The explanation remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unconfirmed

The brief does not establish why the receipt appears in both files.

4. Reviewer: "The matter is referred for specialist ___."

Answer and explanation

C. review

Referral requests review and does not constitute a finding of fraud.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 06: Fraud Indicators and SIU Referral
Original fictional case: Claims analyst Iris notices that invoice reference P71 from the same repair vendor appears in files Q2 and Q4. The team has not established whether this reflects a duplicate upload, an administrative error, or another explanation. Neither the vendor nor the customer has been found to have acted dishonestly. A draft internal note calls the customer fraudulent. Review lead Hassan will correct that language and refer the documented discrepancy through the approved Special Investigation Unit (SIU) route. Original records must be preserved, access restricted to authorized recipients, and no unsupported allegation added to customer correspondence.
Language workshop: Record a discrepancy without labeling the customer
Communication goal: Make an objective specialist referral that separates observed records, suspicion, and findings.
Language guidance: A repeated invoice reference may justify a closer look, but it does not establish who caused it or why. A referral is an action taken because a concern requires review; it is not a finding of fraud.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Invoice P71 appears in both Q2 and Q4 for the same vendor. / We have not established the explanation. / Do not describe the customer as fraudulent. / A duplicate upload or administrative error remains possible. / Retain the original documents and their references. / Refer the discrepancy through the approved SIU route. / Referral is not a finding of fraud. / Correct the case note without concealing its history. / We do not know who caused the repeated entry or why. / Include the two file references and the invoice identifier. / Send the material only to authorized recipients. / Do not insert an unsupported allegation into correspondence. / The concern is awaiting specialist review. / This referral does not itself determine coverage or payment. / Let the authorized reviewers establish the findings. / Record the observed discrepancy, open questions, and referral status.
Vocabulary: Special Investigation Unit (SIU): A specialist function reviewing suspected insurance fraud under the applicable process. / fraud indicator: Information that may warrant examination for possible fraud. / suspicion: A concern that has not been established as a finding. / allegation: An assertion of wrongdoing that requires appropriate evaluation. / verified finding: A conclusion established through the relevant evidential review. / discrepancy: A difference or inconsistency requiring explanation. / duplicate record: A second copy or entry that may repeat existing information. / invoice reference: An identifier assigned to an invoice. / source document: The original or originating record supporting an entry. / administrative error: A mistake in handling or recording information. / referral criteria: The conditions for sending a matter to a specialist review. / referral summary: A concise account of the concern and supporting evidence. / evidence preservation: Retention of relevant material without improper alteration or destruction. / access restriction: A limit on who may view or use information. / authorized recipient: A person permitted to receive the relevant information. / case note: A record of an event, observation, or action in a case file. / source attribution: Identification of where information or an assertion originated. / chronological record: An account organized by the timing of events or entries. / corroboration: Additional evidence supporting a statement or interpretation. / alternative explanation: Another possible account of the observed information. / investigation status: The current stage of an authorized examination. / need-to-know access: Access limited to those requiring the information for their authorized role. / neutral wording: Language describing facts without an unsupported judgment. / review conclusion: The outcome established by the relevant review.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 and distinguish the repeated reference from its unconfirmed explanation. Swap roles for turns 11-20; use the neutral observation rather than the accusatory label. Complete the R88 transfer and check both file references and the review status.
Partner: Iris: Claims analyst / Hassan: Review lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Receipt R88 appears in files H3 and H7. Its explanation is unconfirmed. The matter has been referred for specialist review; no fraud finding exists.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Iris: When another team asks whether the customer has been cleared or found responsible, I should report only the actual review stage.
Hassan: Yes. State the investigation status available through the authorized route. Do not invent either an adverse finding or an exoneration simply because someone wants a short binary answer before the review is complete.
Iris: I will correct the note, preserve both records, restrict access, and submit the documented discrepancy. The explanation remains unconfirmed.
Hassan: Good. Any eventual review conclusion needs its actual basis. Until then, keep observation, possible explanations, referral action, and decision status separate in every record you prepare.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Language guidance: A repeated invoice reference may justify a closer look, but it does not establish who caused it or why. A referral is an action taken because a concern requires review; it is not a finding of fraud.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Invoice P71 appears in both Q2 and Q4 for the same vendor. / We have not established the explanation. / Do not describe the customer as fraudulent. / A duplicate upload or administrative error remains possible. / Retain the original documents and their references. / Refer the discrepancy through the approved SIU route. / Referral is not a finding of fraud. / Correct the case note without concealing its history. / We do not know who caused the repeated entry or why. / Include the two file references and the invoice identifier. / Send the material only to authorized recipients. / Do not insert an unsupported allegation into correspondence. / The concern is awaiting specialist review. / This referral does not itself determine coverage or payment. / Let the authorized reviewers establish the findings. / Record the observed discrepancy, open questions, and referral status.
Vocabulary: Special Investigation Unit (SIU): A specialist function reviewing suspected insurance fraud under the applicable process. / fraud indicator: Information that may warrant examination for possible fraud. / suspicion: A concern that has not been established as a finding. / allegation: An assertion of wrongdoing that requires appropriate evaluation. / verified finding: A conclusion established through the relevant evidential review. / discrepancy: A difference or inconsistency requiring explanation. / duplicate record: A second copy or entry that may repeat existing information. / invoice reference: An identifier assigned to an invoice. / source document: The original or originating record supporting an entry. / administrative error: A mistake in handling or recording information. / referral criteria: The conditions for sending a matter to a specialist review. / referral summary: A concise account of the concern and supporting evidence. / evidence preservation: Retention of relevant material without improper alteration or destruction. / access restriction: A limit on who may view or use information. / authorized recipient: A person permitted to receive the relevant information. / case note: A record of an event, observation, or action in a case file. / source attribution: Identification of where information or an assertion originated. / chronological record: An account organized by the timing of events or entries. / corroboration: Additional evidence supporting a statement or interpretation. / alternative explanation: Another possible account of the observed information. / investigation status: The current stage of an authorized examination. / need-to-know access: Access limited to those requiring the information for their authorized role. / neutral wording: Language describing facts without an unsupported judgment. / review conclusion: The outcome established by the relevant review.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 and distinguish the repeated reference from its unconfirmed explanation. Swap roles for turns 11-20; use the neutral observation rather than the accusatory label. Complete the R88 transfer and check both file references and the review status.
Partner: Iris: Claims analyst / Hassan: Review lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Receipt R88 appears in files H3 and H7. Its explanation is unconfirmed. The matter has been referred for specialist review; no fraud finding exists.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Iris: When another team asks whether the customer has been cleared or found responsible, I should report only the actual review stage.
Hassan: Yes. State the investigation status available through the authorized route. Do not invent either an adverse finding or an exoneration simply because someone wants a short binary answer before the review is complete.
Iris: I will correct the note, preserve both records, restrict access, and submit the documented discrepancy. The explanation remains unconfirmed.
Hassan: Good. Any eventual review conclusion needs its actual basis. Until then, keep observation, possible explanations, referral action, and decision status separate in every record you prepare.
END REFERENCE
07Actuarial Assumptions and PricingFrequency and severity changes multiply+

Your goal

Explain claim-cost movement using consistent exposure, separate drivers, and a limited pricing conclusion.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Frequency and severity changes multiply

Actuarial analyst Pavel compares two fully developed fictional annual claim cohorts with the same 1,000 policy-years of exposure and a consistent claim-count and cost basis. Year 1 has 100 claims averaging $2,000 each. Year 2 has 120 claims averaging $2,200 each. The totals are $200,000 and $264,000. The comparison excludes expenses, reinsurance, investment income, and other pricing components. Manager Farah drafts a note saying costs rose 30%, adding the 20% claim-frequency increase to the 10% severity increase. Pavel must correct the combined change to 32% and explain why this historical comparison does not itself establish a 32% premium increase.

Farah: Portfolio manager / Pavel: Actuarial analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is held constant between the cohorts?

Answer and explanation

D. Exposure at 1,000 policy-years and the stated reporting basis

The brief explicitly holds exposure and the counting and cost basis consistent while claims and severity change.

2. What is Year 2's total claim cost?

Answer and explanation

A. $264,000

One hundred twenty claims multiplied by an average of twenty-two hundred dollars gives two hundred sixty-four thousand.

3. Why is the combined increase not 30%?

Answer and explanation

A. The frequency and severity factors multiply rather than simply add.

The combined factor is 1.20 times 1.10, giving 1.32 and therefore a thirty-two-percent increase.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Frequency rises from 0.10 to 0.12 claims per policy-year: a 20% relative increase. Severity rises from $2,000 to $2,200: 10%. Their combined factor is 1.20 x 1.10 = 1.32, a 32% increase.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
actuary
A professional analyzing financial consequences of risk using relevant methods and standards.consult the actuary
claim frequency
The number of claims per defined unit of exposure.calculate claim frequency
claim severity
The average claim cost on a stated measurement basis.estimate claim severity
policy-year
An exposure unit equivalent to one policy in force for one year on the defined basis.measure exposure in policy-years
exposure base
The quantity used to relate losses or claims to the business at risk.define the exposure base
pure premium
Claim cost per unit of exposure on the stated basis, before other specified components.calculate pure premium
loss cost
A measure of claim cost on a specified basis, distinct from a complete charged premium.estimate loss cost
cohort
A group of policies or claims defined for a common analysis.compare claim cohorts
fully developed claims
Claims whose evaluated costs are treated as complete on the stated analysis basis.compare fully developed claims
development factor
A factor used to estimate change from reported to a later claim-cost stage.assess a development factor
trend
An estimated pattern of change in cost or frequency over time.analyze claim-cost trend
credibility
The weight assigned to information based on its relevance and reliability for estimation.assess statistical credibility
experience period
The period from which observed claims or exposure are drawn.define the experience period
prospective period
The future period for which an estimate is intended.specify the prospective period
rate indication
An actuarial estimate informing a proposed rate level under stated assumptions.develop a rate indication
expense provision
An allowance for defined expense components in an estimate or price.include an expense provision
reinsurance cost
The cost associated with transferring specified insurance risk to another insurer.account for reinsurance cost
loss ratio
Loss amounts divided by the specified premium measure on a stated basis.define the loss ratio
combined ratio
A measure combining defined loss and expense ratios under the stated convention.interpret the combined ratio
multiplicative effect
A combined change obtained by multiplying relative factors.explain the multiplicative effect
percentage-point change
The arithmetic difference between two percentages.distinguish a percentage-point change
sensitivity test
An evaluation of how results vary with changed assumptions.conduct a sensitivity test
model assumption
A condition or value adopted in an analytical model.disclose a model assumption
pricing recommendation
A proposed price decision based on the relevant analysis and constraints.support a pricing recommendation

Pure premium on the stated claim-cost basis rises from $200 to $264 per policy-year. This is not the charged premium or a complete forward-looking rate indication. Other pricing components and assumptions remain outside the example.

C Notice the language

Correct arithmetic while protecting the decision context

A manager may compress a technical result into an apparently simple price instruction. Show the calculation in ordinary language, name the exposure basis, and separate historical claim experience from a complete forward-looking price recommendation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Define the population
Both cohorts contain 1,000 policy-years of exposure.
State the count change
Claims increased from 100 to 120.
Name the rate
Frequency rose from 0.10 to 0.12 claims per policy-year.
Quantify the relative change
That is a 20% frequency increase.
Define severity
Average claim cost rose from $2,000 to $2,200.
Quantify the second driver
Severity increased by 10%.
Combine the factors
Multiply 1.20 by 1.10 to get 1.32.
Correct the headline
Total claim cost rose 32%, not 30%.
Show the amounts
Costs increased from $200,000 to $264,000.
State the exposure-normalized result
Pure premium rose from $200 to $264 per policy-year.
Keep the basis consistent
The claim-count and cost bases are the same.
Name the exclusions
Expenses, reinsurance, and other pricing components are excluded.
Avoid a price instruction
This does not establish a 32% premium increase.
Separate past and future
Historical experience is an input, not a complete forecast.
Ask for the full analysis
A pricing recommendation needs the relevant prospective assumptions.
Close with the calculation
Show the exposure, frequency, severity, and combined factor together.
6 language and precision notes
Per policy-year
Supplies the exposure denominator needed for a frequency measure.
Increased by versus increased to
Distinguishes the amount of change from the new level.
Relative increase
Compares the change with the starting value.
Multiply versus add
Combined proportional drivers produce an interaction effect.
On this basis
Limits the claim-cost measure to the supplied definitions and exclusions.
Indication versus instruction
Analysis informs a decision; one historical metric does not dictate it.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. What is Year 1's frequency?

Answer and explanation

A. 0.10 claims per policy-year

One hundred claims divided by one thousand policy-years gives 0.10 claims per policy-year.

2. What is the relative total-cost increase?

Answer and explanation

A. 32%

The sixty-four-thousand increase divided by the two-hundred-thousand starting total equals thirty-two percent.

3. What is Year 2's pure premium on this basis?

Answer and explanation

C. $264 per policy-year

Dividing the two-hundred-sixty-four-thousand total by one thousand policy-years gives two hundred sixty-four per policy-year.

4. Which price conclusion is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. This historical comparison alone does not determine a premium change.

The analysis excludes other pricing components and prospective assumptions, so it cannot prescribe a complete price change.

E Conversations

Frequency and severity changes multiply

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Farah: Portfolio manager / Pavel: Actuarial analyst

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  • pricing recommendation

  1. Farah
    My summary says claim costs rose thirty percent: frequency increased twenty percent and severity ten percent. Is that the correct combined figure?
  2. Pavel
    It is thirty-two percent. 1 rose from 0.10 to 0.12 per policy-year. Severity also increased, so we multiply the two relative factors rather than simply add their percentage changes.
  3. Farah
    Before we show the calculation, confirm that both groups have the same exposure. Otherwise claim counts alone could give a misleading comparison.
  4. Pavel
    Each has one thousand 2. The claim-count and cost bases are also consistent. That lets us compare the supplied frequency figures without attributing an exposure increase to a change in underlying claim frequency.
  5. Farah
    Year 1 has a hundred claims at two thousand dollars each. Year 2 has a hundred twenty at twenty-two hundred each.
  6. Pavel
    Correct. 3 increases ten percent. Total claim cost therefore moves from two hundred thousand to two hundred sixty-four thousand dollars. The difference is sixty-four thousand, not sixty thousand.
  7. Farah
    So the total-cost ratio is 264,000 divided by 200,000, or 1.32. Subtracting one gives the thirty-two-percent relative increase.
  8. Pavel
    Exactly. The 4 is 1.20 times 1.10. More claims also experience the higher average cost. Adding twenty and ten omits that interaction and understates the combined change.
  9. Farah
    Can we express the result per exposure unit as well? The portfolio team is used to seeing the amount per policy-year.
  10. Pavel
    On this basis, 5 rises from two hundred to two hundred sixty-four dollars per policy-year. Keep the definition attached: this is the stated claim-cost measure, not the full premium charged to a customer.
  11. Farah
    The pricing team will ask whether thirty-two percent is our proposed rate increase. I need to separate the finding from that recommendation.
  12. Pavel
    It does not establish a complete 6. Expenses, reinsurance, and other pricing components are excluded. We also need the relevant prospective assumptions rather than assume that this historical change dictates the next year's price.
  13. Farah
    The case calls both cohorts fully developed. That avoids comparing one mature group with another group whose claims have barely been reported.
  14. Pavel
    Yes. 7 are an explicit assumption here. In another dataset, differences in development or reporting maturity would require attention. We must not carry this convenient assumption into a real portfolio without evidence.
  15. Farah
    These historical annual groups are not automatically a forecast of the year we are about to price.
  16. Pavel
    Name the 8 and distinguish it from the prospective period. Historical experience can inform a forecast, but it does not guarantee future frequency, severity, or the mix of business.
  17. Farah
    I will label the excluded costs explicitly. An empty expense line could otherwise make this look like a complete price calculation.
  18. Pavel
    Correct. An 9 and other relevant components need their own basis in a complete analysis. A clean arithmetic example should not silently become a model with missing costs treated as nonexistent.
  19. Farah
    The revised slide will show equal exposure, separate frequency and severity changes, the multiplying factors, and the thirty-two-percent total-cost increase.
  20. Pavel
    Good. Keep any 10 separate until the broader work supports it. This correction improves the evidence presented to the decision-makers without claiming that one historical percentage answers every pricing question.

Answers and explanations
  1. Claim frequency Turn 2. Claim frequency divides claim count by exposure; with exposure unchanged, the rise from 100 to 120 claims is twenty percent.
  2. policy-years Turn 4. Policy-years provide the common exposure unit; both cohorts use one thousand on the supplied consistent basis.
  3. Claim severity Turn 6. Claim severity is average claim cost; increasing from two thousand to twenty-two hundred dollars is ten percent.
  4. multiplicative effect Turn 8. The multiplicative effect combines 1.20 and 1.10 as 1.32, including the interaction missed by adding twenty and ten.
  5. pure premium Turn 10. Pure premium expresses the stated claim cost per exposure unit, not the full charged insurance premium.
  6. rate indication Turn 12. A rate indication requires relevant prospective cost analysis; the historical claim-cost change excludes necessary pricing components and assumptions.
  7. Fully developed claims Turn 14. Fully developed claims are treated as complete on the supplied analysis basis, keeping development differences out of this particular comparison.
  8. experience period Turn 16. The experience period identifies when observed data arose; it is separate from the future period a pricing estimate addresses.
  9. expense provision Turn 18. An expense provision accounts for defined expenses; excluding it from the exercise does not establish that actual expenses are zero.
  10. pricing recommendation Turn 20. A pricing recommendation requires the broader relevant analysis; the corrected historical arithmetic is an input rather than a final instruction.

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

More claims and costlier claims

Fictional actuarial explanation: comparable exposure periods show both increased claim frequency and increased average claim severity; no pricing decision is supplied.

  1. Portfolio manager: Claim costs increased. Can you explain how both frequency and severity contributed in this report?
  2. Actuarial analyst: Frequency concerns how often claims occur relative to the exposure basis; severity concerns average cost per claim.
  3. Portfolio manager: So more claims and more expensive claims are different drivers, even when total costs rise together.
  4. Actuarial analyst: Exactly. The report says both increased on its comparable basis, rather than attributing everything to one driver.
  5. Portfolio manager: Does higher severity mean every individual claim became more expensive than every claim last year?
  6. Actuarial analyst: No. An average doesn't describe each claim, and the mix can affect that summary.
  7. Portfolio manager: I'll explain the two drivers separately and include the comparison period and exposure basis.
  8. Actuarial analyst: Good. Keep any pricing recommendation separate from this descriptive explanation of the observed claim experience.
  9. Portfolio manager: Should reserve adequacy be declared from those two increases alone during the meeting?
  10. Actuarial analyst: No. Reserve assessment requires its own analysis and assumptions, including relevant loss-development information.
Paid so far is not the final claim cost

Separate fictional actuarial briefing: a recent claim cohort has paid amounts recorded, but some claims remain open and development assumptions need review.

  1. Finance reporting manager: The paid total is available. Why doesn't the actuarial report call it the final cost?
  2. Reserving analyst: Some claims remain open, so amounts paid so far don't establish their eventual total cost.
  3. Finance reporting manager: Is loss development the way reported claim costs change as more information becomes available?
  4. Reserving analyst: Yes, broadly. The specific analysis and assumptions need to be explained for this cohort.
  5. Finance reporting manager: Then a current payment total is an observation, while an ultimate-cost estimate includes assessed future development.
  6. Reserving analyst: Correct. We should label those figures separately instead of presenting an estimate as settled fact.
  7. Finance reporting manager: Could the lower paid total for recent claims prove their severity is lower than older claims?
  8. Reserving analyst: Not without accounting for maturity and comparable definitions. Recent claims have had less time to develop.
  9. Finance reporting manager: I'll request the matched comparison and preserve the estimate's assumptions in the briefing.
  10. Reserving analyst: Good. That clarifies why paid amounts alone don't determine final cost or reserve adequacy.
Claim counts need an exposure denominator

Separate fictional portfolio comparison: A records 20 claims across 1,000 policy-years; B records 30 across 3,000 policy-years, with comparable definitions assumed for this exercise.

  1. Pricing trainee: Portfolio B has thirty claims and A has twenty. Is B's frequency higher?
  2. Actuarial tutor: Not on this exposure basis. A has one thousand policy-years; B has three thousand.
  3. Pricing trainee: So we compare claims per policy-year, rather than only the raw number of claims.
  4. Actuarial tutor: Exactly. These figures give twenty per thousand for A and ten per thousand for B.
  5. Pricing trainee: B has more claims in total but a lower frequency relative to the supplied exposure.
  6. Actuarial tutor: Correct. That distinction matters whenever portfolio sizes differ in an actuarial comparison.
  7. Pricing trainee: Does the lower frequency also establish lower severity or a lower appropriate premium for B?
  8. Actuarial tutor: No. We haven't supplied claim amounts or the other information needed for pricing analysis.
  9. Pricing trainee: I'll state the frequency result only, using the same denominator and clearly defined period.
  10. Actuarial tutor: Good. Keep frequency, severity, and any reserve or pricing conclusions distinct instead of extending one calculation.

Combine another pair of claim drivers

Two cohorts have the same exposure. Frequency increases 10% and severity increases 20%. Starting total claim cost is $100,000; all other calculation conditions are held constant.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The frequency factor is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 1.10

A ten-percent relative increase corresponds to multiplying the starting frequency by 1.10.

2. Manager: "The severity factor is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 1.20

A twenty-percent relative increase corresponds to multiplying starting severity by 1.20.

3. Analyst: "The combined factor is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 1.32

Multiplying 1.10 by 1.20 gives 1.32, not an additive factor of 1.30.

4. Manager: "The resulting claim-cost total is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. 132,000

One hundred thousand multiplied by 1.32 equals one hundred thirty-two thousand dollars.

Farah: The revised slide will show equal exposure, separate frequency and severity changes, the multiplying factors, and the thirty-two-percent total-cost increase.
Pavel: Good. Keep any pricing recommendation separate until the broader work supports it. This correction improves the evidence presented to the decision-makers without claiming that one historical percentage answers every pricing question.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

Lesson 07: Actuarial Assumptions and Pricing
Original fictional case: Actuarial analyst Pavel compares two fully developed fictional annual claim cohorts with the same 1,000 policy-years of exposure and a consistent claim-count and cost basis. Year 1 has 100 claims averaging $2,000 each. Year 2 has 120 claims averaging $2,200 each. The totals are $200,000 and $264,000. The comparison excludes expenses, reinsurance, investment income, and other pricing components. Manager Farah drafts a note saying costs rose 30%, adding the 20% claim-frequency increase to the 10% severity increase. Pavel must correct the combined change to 32% and explain why this historical comparison does not itself establish a 32% premium increase.
Language workshop: Frequency and severity changes multiply
Communication goal: Explain claim-cost movement using consistent exposure, separate drivers, and a limited pricing conclusion.
Language guidance: Frequency rises from 0.10 to 0.12 claims per policy-year: a 20% relative increase. Severity rises from $2,000 to $2,200: 10%. Their combined factor is 1.20 x 1.10 = 1.32, a 32% increase.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Both cohorts contain 1,000 policy-years of exposure. / Claims increased from 100 to 120. / Frequency rose from 0.10 to 0.12 claims per policy-year. / That is a 20% frequency increase. / Average claim cost rose from $2,000 to $2,200. / Severity increased by 10%. / Multiply 1.20 by 1.10 to get 1.32. / Total claim cost rose 32%, not 30%. / Costs increased from $200,000 to $264,000. / Pure premium rose from $200 to $264 per policy-year. / The claim-count and cost bases are the same. / Expenses, reinsurance, and other pricing components are excluded. / This does not establish a 32% premium increase. / Historical experience is an input, not a complete forecast. / A pricing recommendation needs the relevant prospective assumptions. / Show the exposure, frequency, severity, and combined factor together.
Vocabulary: actuary: A professional analyzing financial consequences of risk using relevant methods and standards. / claim frequency: The number of claims per defined unit of exposure. / claim severity: The average claim cost on a stated measurement basis. / policy-year: An exposure unit equivalent to one policy in force for one year on the defined basis. / exposure base: The quantity used to relate losses or claims to the business at risk. / pure premium: Claim cost per unit of exposure on the stated basis, before other specified components. / loss cost: A measure of claim cost on a specified basis, distinct from a complete charged premium. / cohort: A group of policies or claims defined for a common analysis. / fully developed claims: Claims whose evaluated costs are treated as complete on the stated analysis basis. / development factor: A factor used to estimate change from reported to a later claim-cost stage. / trend: An estimated pattern of change in cost or frequency over time. / credibility: The weight assigned to information based on its relevance and reliability for estimation. / experience period: The period from which observed claims or exposure are drawn. / prospective period: The future period for which an estimate is intended. / rate indication: An actuarial estimate informing a proposed rate level under stated assumptions. / expense provision: An allowance for defined expense components in an estimate or price. / reinsurance cost: The cost associated with transferring specified insurance risk to another insurer. / loss ratio: Loss amounts divided by the specified premium measure on a stated basis. / combined ratio: A measure combining defined loss and expense ratios under the stated convention. / multiplicative effect: A combined change obtained by multiplying relative factors. / percentage-point change: The arithmetic difference between two percentages. / sensitivity test: An evaluation of how results vary with changed assumptions. / model assumption: A condition or value adopted in an analytical model. / pricing recommendation: A proposed price decision based on the relevant analysis and constraints.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, saying the exposure denominator and multiplying 1.20 by 1.10 aloud. Swap roles for turns 11-20; contrast historical claim cost with a prospective price. Complete the second-cohort transfer and check its factors and $132,000 total.
Partner: Farah: Portfolio manager / Pavel: Actuarial analyst
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Two cohorts have the same exposure. Frequency increases 10% and severity increases 20%. Starting total claim cost is $100,000; all other calculation conditions are held constant.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Farah: I will label the excluded costs explicitly. An empty expense line could otherwise make this look like a complete price calculation.
Pavel: Correct. An expense provision and other relevant components need their own basis in a complete analysis. A clean arithmetic example should not silently become a model with missing costs treated as nonexistent.
Farah: The revised slide will show equal exposure, separate frequency and severity changes, the multiplying factors, and the thirty-two-percent total-cost increase.
Pavel: Good. Keep any pricing recommendation separate until the broader work supports it. This correction improves the evidence presented to the decision-makers without claiming that one historical percentage answers every pricing question.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
